L50: ## 1. Executive Summary
L51: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L52: Berotralstat is an orally administered inhibitor of active plasma kallikrein for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 12 years of age and older. Berotralstat is a new molecular entity (NME) and not approved for use in any country. The dosing regimen for approval consists of a recommended starting dose of 150 mg orally once daily and 110 mg orally once daily for patients with moderate-to-severe hepatic impairment or for patients with persistent gastrointestinal (GI) adverse reactions.
L53: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L54: The recommended regulatory action for this application is Approval of orally administered berotralstat 150 mg once daily for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE in patients 12 years of age and older. To support this application, the Applicant conducted a single 24-week, adequate, well-controlled pivotal efficacy and safety trial in adolescents and adults with type I or II HAE. The results from this trial demonstrated evidence of efficacy for both the 110 mg and 150 mg doses of berotralstat compared to placebo for the primary endpoint of HAE attack rate over 24 weeks and the predefined exploratory endpoint of a \ge 50\% reduction in attack rate compared to baseline. The study had adequate statistical power to detect a clinically meaningful difference between berotralstat treatment versus placebo. Based on the robustness of the results and the rarity of the disease, a single pivotal trial is adequate to demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness. The Division did not deem the results from the first key secondary endpoint, the angioedema quality-of-life (AE-QoL) questionnaire score, fit for purpose for labeling. Nevertheless the results were not statistically significant versus placebo for either treatment group and further secondary endpoints were therefore not tested because of the hierarchical analysis. The numerically greater and more clinically significant treatment effect in the high-dose arm supports a recommended dose of 150 mg once daily. However, the totality of safety and efficacy data also support use of the 110 mg once-daily dose for patients who may not tolerate the higher dose due to GI-related adverse reactions or who have moderate to severe hepatic impairment.
L55: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L56: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L57: Berotralstat is an orally administered inhibitor of active plasma kallikrein for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 12 years of age and older. The recommended regulatory action of this NDA is Approval.
L58: Hereditary angioedema (HAE) is a rare, autosomal dominant, inherited condition characterized by intermittent, unpredictable attacks of angioedema. HAE is caused by mutations in the *SERPING1* gene, resulting in deficient or dysfunctional C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH) protein, a serine protease inhibitor. Absence of functional C1-INH leads to dysregulation of the contact system, unchecked kallikrein activity, and ultimately widespread release of bradykinin, which increases vascular permeability causing the characteristic swelling of acute HAE attacks. Although the exact prevalence is unknown, HAE is estimated to affect 1 in 10,000 to 50,000 individuals worldwide (approximately 6,000 to 10,000 individuals in the United States) and is categorized as an orphan disease. Acute attacks of HAE are potentially life-threatening, particularly in cases of laryngeal edema resulting in airway compromise. Attacks at other anatomic sites (e.g., gastrointestinal [GI] tract, genitourinary tract, and skin on the hands, feet, and face) can cause disabling pain and significant morbidity. These attacks are highly variable in frequency and location among individuals and even within a given individual. Patients with particularly severe and/or frequent attacks may be treated with prophylactic therapies to prevent or reduce acute attacks. While the context of available therapies for prophylaxis as well as treatment of acute attacks has expanded greatly in the last decade, most therapies require subcutaneous injection or intravenous infusion. Danazol, an attenuated androgen, is the only oral therapy approved for HAE prophylaxis; however, safety and tolerability issues limit its use.
L59: The efficacy and safety of berotralstat was evaluated in a single, well-controlled, adequately designed pivotal trial in Type I and II HAE patients 12 years of age and older. Patients had a minimum of 2 HAE attacks over a 2-month run-in period. Berotralstat treatment for 24 weeks versus placebo resulted in statistically significant and clinically meaningful reductions in the rate of HAE attacks. The 110 and 150 mg doses of berotralstat reduced the rate of HAE attacks by 30% (p=0.024) and 44% (p&lt;0.001) relative to placebo, respectively. Efficacy was further supported by additional endpoints such as proportion of patients with at least a 50% reduction in HAE attacks compared to baseline. While the study demonstrated a significant treatment benefit in a patient population with frequent attacks who are likely to require prophylaxis, the treatment response was modest as few subjects achieved complete elimination of attacks. However, an oral therapy provides an additional, convenient option to the treatment armamentarium.
L60: The safety data submitted with the NDA were sufficient to assess the safety of berotralstat in the proposed HAE population. Gastrointestinal-related adverse events and transaminase elevations emerged as the main safety signals. Adverse reactions such as abdominal pain, vomiting, diarrhea, dyspepsia and gastroesophageal reflux occurred more frequently, often in a dose-
L61: dependent manner, with berotralstat compared to placebo. However, GI-related events were generally mild-to-moderate severity and self-limited, and typically did not lead to study drug discontinuation. Adverse events related to transaminase elevations, primarily increased ALT, were reported with berotralstat treatment in the controlled and open label extension studies. Few subjects had marked elevations triggering treatment discontinuation, and no potential Hy's law cases were identified in the program. Transaminase elevations were generally transient, asymptomatic, not dose-dependent, and in some cases confounded by underlying co-morbidities and prior androgen use. In general, safety data from the open label extension study were consistent with the placebo controlled data and support chronic berotralstat use. Although not observed in clinical trials, QT prolongation is an anticipated risk with higher than recommended doses based on the pharmacologic action of the drug. No safety concerns were identified that should preclude approval or require a REMS.
L62: Efficacy and safety results across various demographic and baseline characteristic subgroups were generally consistent with the overall findings. Given the small number of adolescent subjects, efficacy in this age group is primarily based on extrapolation from efficacy data in adults because the pathophysiology of disease is similar irrespective of age. The numerically greater and more clinically significant treatment effect in the high-dose arm supports a recommended dose of 150 mg once daily. However, the totality of safety and efficacy data also support use of the 110 mg once-daily dose for patients who may not tolerate the higher dose due to GI-related adverse reactions or who have moderate to severe hepatic impairment. Overall, the risk/benefit profile for berotralstat in the treatment of a rare, serious disease such as HAE is favorable.

L71: ## 2. Therapeutic Context
L72: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L73: Hereditary angioedema (HAE) is a rare, autosomal dominant, inherited condition characterized by intermittent, unpredictable attacks of angioedema. HAE is caused by mutations in the *SERPING1* gene, resulting in deficiency or dysfunction of C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH) protein, a serine protease inhibitor. Approximately 85% of patients have type I HAE, characterized by low production of normal C1-INH protein; the remaining 15% of patients have type II HAE, characterized by normal production and levels of dysfunctional C1-INH. The absence of functional C1-INH leads to dysregulation of the contact system, a plasma protease cascade initiated by factor XII that activates the proinflammatory kallikrein-kinin system (Figure 1) and the procoagulant intrinsic coagulation pathway. Ordinarily, kallikrein activity is regulated by C1-INH, but in patients with HAE, kallikrein activity is unchecked, leading to widespread release of bradykinin. The resulting elevated level of bradykinin increases vascular permeability, leading to the characteristic swelling of acute HAE attacks.
L74: Figure 1 provides a more detailed representation of how C1-INH functions within the contact, complement, and fibrinolytic systems. C1-INH is an endogenous inhibitor of the serine protease enzymes Factor XIIa and plasma kallikrein. Factor XIIa converts prekallikrein to active plasma kallikrein, which in turn cleaves high-molecular-weight kininogen (HMWK) to form bradykinin and cleaved HMWK. Bradykinin is a polypeptide signaling molecule that participates in multiple biological processes, including blood coagulation, activation of inflammation, and regulation of blood pressure.
L75: Patients with HAE have uncontrolled plasma kallikrein production and pathologic levels of bradykinin. The key triggering events of HAE attacks are not completely understood. Activation by bradykinin of bradykinin B2 receptors on the surface of endothelial cells leads to vasodilation and decreased blood pressure. Activation of B2 receptors also results in the vascular leakage and subsequent tissue swelling characteristic of HAE attacks. Bradykinin is converted to des-Arg9 bradykinin in the plasma by carboxypeptidase N. Des-Arg9 bradykinin activates bradykinin B1 receptors, thus contributing to symptoms of HAE. Berotralstat is designed to block the enzymatic function of plasma kallikrein and so prevent the proteolytic conversion of HMWK to bradykinin1.
L76: 1 Kenniston, JA, RR Faucette, D Martik, SR Comeau, AP Lindberg, KJ Kopacz, GP Conley, J Chen, M Viswanathan, N Kastrapeli, J Cosic, S Mason, M DiLeo, J Abendroth, P Kuzmic, RC Ladner, TE Edwards, C TenHoor, BA Adelman, AE Nixon, and DJ Sexton, 2014, Inhibition of plasma kallikrein by a highly specific active site blocking antibody, *J Biol Chem*, 289(34):23596-23608; and Zuraw, BL, JA Bernstein, DM Lang, T Craig, D Dreyfus, F Hsieh, D Khan, J Sheikh, D Weldon, DI Bernstein, J Blessing-Moore, L Cox, RA Nicklas, J Oppenheimer, JM Portnoy, CR Randolph, DE Schuller, SL Spector, SA Tilles, and D Wallace, 2013, A focused parameter update: hereditary angioedema, acquired C1 inhibitor deficiency, and angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor-associated angioedema, *J Allergy Clin Immunol*, 131(6):1491-1493.

L78: Source: World Allergy Organization Global Hereditary Angioedema Practice Parameter
Abbreviations: C, complement; C1-INH, C1-esterase inhibitor; HMW-K, high-molecular-weight kininogen
L79: Although the exact prevalence is unknown, HAE is estimated to affect 1 in 10,000 to 50,000 individuals worldwide (approximately 6,000 to 10,000 individuals in the United States) and is categorized as an orphan disease. Despite its autosomal dominant inheritance pattern, roughly one quarter of patients with HAE have spontaneous mutations, and diagnosis is often delayed by several years. The age at which attacks begin is variable, but symptoms during infancy are exceedingly rare. Based on literature reports, the median age of symptom onset is between 6 and 11 years. The attack frequency increases after puberty and continues throughout an individual's life. The acute attacks of HAE are potentially life-threatening, particularly in cases of laryngeal edema resulting in airway compromise. Attacks at other anatomic sites (e.g., gastrointestinal [GI] tract, genitourinary tract, hands, feet, and face) can cause disabling pain and significant morbidity. These attacks are highly variable in frequency and location both among individuals and within a given individual. If untreated, attacks may occur on average every 1 to 2 weeks and typically last 48 to 72 hours. The therapies currently used by patients with HAE to manage their disease are listed in Section 2.2.
L80: The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) convened a Patient-Focused Drug Development public meeting for HAE on September 25, 2017, during which FDA staff heard directly from patients, families, and caregivers about their experiences with HAE attacks and the life-changing impact of available therapies. Approximately 110 patients with HAE and their representatives were present at the meeting and a further 52 participated by means of a live webcast. The discussion at the meeting focused on two key topics: 1) the disease symptoms and daily impacts that matter most to patients and caregivers and 2) patients' perspectives on current approaches to treating HAE and on participating in clinical studies. The *Voice of the Patient* report highlighted the following overarching themes from the discussion that took place during the meeting:
L81: - Diagnostic delays and lack of awareness of HAE and recognition of signs/symptoms by healthcare providers.
- Unpredictability of attacks.
- Pain, disability, and disfigurement that result from attacks.
- Emotional stress that accompanies the condition.
- Impact on work, family, and social life.
- Life-changing effect of newer approved treatments.
L82: - Unmet need for treatments that are less invasive, improve predictability of attacks, have fewer side effects, and ultimately lead to cure.
- Areas for future research include hormonal influences on the disease, therapies for children, and therapies for HAE types that are less responsive to currently available therapies.
- Willingness to participate in clinical trials and natural history studies.
L83: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L84: Medications used to treat HAE patients are typically categorized as treatments for acute attacks or prophylaxis. Although currently available treatments (Table 1) for routine prophylaxis of acute HAE attacks are effective in reducing their number and frequency, they do not eliminate all attacks in every individual. While fresh frozen plasma and antifibrinolytics (tranexamic acid, \epsilon-aminocaproic acid) are available for off-label use in HAE, their use has fallen out of favor following the availability of effective FDA-approved therapies.2

L92: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L93: Berotralstat is not approved or marketed in the United States or any foreign country for any other indication.
L94: ## 3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L95: Berotralstat (BCX7353) was developed under investigational new drug (IND) application 135058, which was opened on June 22, 2017. Prior to opening the IND, the Applicant had multiple ex-US clinical studies (completed and ongoing) evaluating the use of berotralstat for prevention of HAE attacks. Berotralstat was granted orphan therapy designation on November 1, 2017, and fast-track designation on August 2, 2018. Relevant interactions between the Agency and Applicant to discuss the clinical development program are summarized below.
L96: *End-of-Phase 2 meeting: November 2, 2017*
L97: - Agreement to submit the final report of 2-year carcinogenicity study of berotralstat in rats prior to submitting NDA
- Discussed that a thorough QTc study may be required
- Disagreement with proposed study design and safety database for a single trial to support an NDA. Six months of blinded, controlled data will be required to support safety of an NME. Provided no major safety issues are identified in the phase 3 trial, the safety database for this program should include approximately 64 to 70 patients in the controlled portion of the study and approximately 100 subjects exposed for at least 1 year
L98: - Approach to dose selection for phase 3 was reasonable; however, the Division could not comment further given that the 150 mg dose had not been previously studied. Advised against dose titration and modifications within the study as this may complicate the safety evaluation and result in insufficient long-term safety data to support the proposed dose
- Exclusion criteria should be limited where possible to ensure generalizability of results
- Primary efficacy endpoint of investigator-confirmed attack rate over 24 weeks acceptable; however, disagreement with proposed definition of HAE attacks. HAE attack definition should require presence of swelling, and premature use of rescue medication to treat prodromal symptoms should not be considered an acute attack. For completeness, all attacks should be captured in the safety database
L99: *Pre-NDA meeting: May 29, 2019*
L100: - Agreement that the food effect had been adequately studied
- Agreement with proposed format and content of NDA submission
- Preliminary agreement that safety summary data did not suggest need for a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) plan in the initial NDA submission
- Size and scope of the safety database appeared adequate to support NDA filing and review
L101: Of note, the Applicant has a related IND 142879 for the treatment of acute HAE attacks with berotralstat. The higher proposed doses for treatment of acute HAE attacks generated concern regarding the potential for significant QT prolongation, particularly if used on top of prophylaxis therapy. The FDA communicated these concerns to the Applicant and further stated that addressing the issue of QT prolongation was an essential component of the development program of berotralstat for the treatment of acute HAE attacks (refer to Meeting Minutes dated August 20, 2019).
L102: ## 4. Significant Issues From Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to the Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L103: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations
L104: The pivotal efficacy and safety trial, Study BCX7352-302 Part 1, in the berotralstat development program was a multicenter trial in which each site enrolled a small number of subjects. Therefore, the potential for any individual study center to impact the efficacy and safety findings was limited. Two clinical sites (Dr. Johnston/Site #033 and Dr. Lumry/Site #035) were selected for inspection for Study BCX7352-302 Part 1. Both sites were chosen for having a relatively large enrollment and higher response rates on the primary efficacy endpoint compared to other sites in the United States. The Office of Scientific Investigations reports for these sites found them to be in compliance with Good Clinical Practices and their study data to be reliably supportive of the requested indication.
L105: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L106: CMC information for two strengths (110 and 150 mg) of berotralstat capsules were submitted to the NDA. [REDACTED](b) (4) there were two distinct drug product manufacturing sites. Both strengths have been evaluated from a quality perspective. The proposed drug product specification is appropriate and includes testing and acceptance criteria for description, identification (HPLC and UV), assay, degradation products, content uniformity, dissolution, [REDACTED](b) (4) and microbial limits, as recommended by ICH Q6A. Impurities specification acceptance criteria followed the recommendations of ICH Q3B. The associated analytical methods were found to be suitable for their intended use. There are no plans for specific tests for elemental impurities in the drug product, and this is supported by the risk assessment and data from 12 registration lots. The applicant's request for a categorical exclusion from the requirements to submit an environmental assessment per 21 CFR 25.31(b) is acceptable. All the facilities have been approved based on previous history and with concurrence with ORA recommendations. Based on the drug solubility, drug dissolution rate of the pivotal clinical and registration batches for both strengths of berotralstat capsules, low variability of drug release with the proposed dissolution method, the Applicant's proposed dissolution method and acceptance criterion are considered adequate. The overall recommendation for this product is approval from a product quality perspective.

L111: ## 5. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L112: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L113: The nonclinical program of berotralstat consists of studies of its pharmacology, pharmacokinetics (PK), and toxicology. Pharmacology studies showed that berotralstat inhibited kallikrein activity across species. PK studies showed that orally administered berotralstat is bioavailable in animals and humans. There are no major and unique berotralstat metabolites in humans. Toxicology studies evaluated the general toxicity, genetic toxicity, carcinogenicity, and reproductive and developmental toxicity of berotralstat. General toxicity studies included chronic oral toxicity studies in rats and monkeys (treatment durations of up to 26 and 39 weeks, respectively). Genetic toxicity was evaluated by means of a standard battery of assays. Carcinogenicity studies included studies of oral berotralstat with a traditional 2-year bioassay in rats and a 26-week study in transgenic mice. Reproductive and developmental
L114: studies included teratology studies in rats and rabbits, a fertility and early embryonic developmental study in rats, and a peri- and post-natal development (PPND) study in rats.
L115: ## 5.1.1. Carcinogenicity
L116: No evidence of tumorigenicity was found in mice or rats. The carcinogenicity potential of berotralstat was evaluated in a 2-year traditional bioassay in rats and a 26-week study in B6F1 Tg.RasH2 mice. Wistar rats (60/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 3, 8 and 20 mg/kg berotralstat once daily for 104 weeks. Mice (25/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 8, 20, and 50 mg/kg berotralstat once daily for 26 weeks. Neither study revealed treatment-related increases in tumor incidences.
L117: ## 5.1.2. Genetic Toxicity
L118: Berotralstat produced negative results in the bacterial reverse gene mutation assay, an in vitro chromosomal aberration assay in human peripheral blood lymphocytes, and an in vivo micronucleus assay in rats.
L119: ## 5.1.3. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity
L120: Berotralstat was nonteratogenic in pregnant rats and rabbits dosed with the drug during the organogenesis period. Berotralstat did not affect the fertility or PPND of rats.
L121: ## 5.1.4. Others
L122: Toxicity studies indicated that berotralstat can result in phospholipidosis and affect the cardiovascular (CV) system of animals.
L123: ## 5.1.4.1. Phospholipidosis
L124: Repeat-dose administration of berotralstat can cause phospholipidosis in rats and monkeys. Phospholipidosis-related findings included vacuoles in the epithelium and mucosa of the intestine, kidney, and choroid plexus (rats); foamy macrophages and histiocytes in the spleen, thymus, and intestine; and foamy Kupffer cells in the liver. Phospholipidosis was confirmed by electronic microscopic examination of Kupffer cells (myelinosomes) in the liver and the urinary level of di-22:6-bis(monoacylglycol) phosphate, a biomarker of phospholipidosis. Phospholipidosis is a known drug-storage mechanism. There is no evidence that berotralstat causes phospholipidosis in humans.
L125: ## 5.1.4.2. Cardiovascular Effects
L126: Berotralstat prolonged the QT interval in monkeys and humans. Berotralstat affected the conductance of potassium and sodium channels and the membrane action potential in vitro. Berotralstat also affected blood pressure, heart rate, and electrocardiogram (ECG) (i.e., slight increases in the RR, PR, QT, and QTc intervals, and in the QRS duration) in monkeys. In humans, berotralstat at an oral dose of 450 mg (maximum concentration [Cmax] 191 ng/mL) prolongs the QT interval by approximately 16 ms. A thorough QT study of the effect of berotralstat on the QT interval in humans has been completed.
L127: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, Biologics License Applications, and Drug Master Files
L128: - Biologics license application (BLA) 125277—KALBITOR (ecallantide), a recombinant human plasma kallikrein inhibitor indicated for HAE; approved on December 1, 2009.
- BLA 761090—TAKHZYRO (lanadelumab); approved on August 23, 2018.
- NDA 022150—FIRAZYR (icatibant acetate injectable); approved on August 25, 2011.
- IND 135058—Berotralstat, intended for the prevention of HAE attacks.
- IND 142879—Berotralstat, intended for the treatment of HAE attacks.
L129: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L130: Berotralstat decreases vascular permeability and edema in patients with HAE by reducing the blood level of bradykinin, a potent vasodilator. Berotralstat decreases the bradykinin level by inhibiting kallikrein, a plasma serine protease responsible for bradykinin production. Kallikrein cleaves and releases bradykinin from HMWK. Kallikrein is inactivated by C1-INH. Patients with HAE exhibit excessive kallikrein activity as a result of C1-INH deficiency or dysfunction.
L131: ## 5.3.1. Primary Pharmacology
L132: Berotralstat inhibited plasma kallikrein from animals and humans. The half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC_{50}) was 5.2, 5.7, and 5.4nM for rat, monkey, and human kallikrein (healthy subjects), respectively (Study BR-7353-005-REV-01). Kallikrein from patients with HAE was less sensitive to inhibition by berotralstat than was that from healthy subjects. This was indicated by a three-fold difference in IC_{50} (15.9 and 5.4nM for kallikrein from patients with HAE and healthy subjects, respectively; Study BR-7353-006-REV-01). Berotralstat has an inhibition constant of 0.44nM for isolated/purified human plasma kallikrein (Study BR-7353-001), but the IC_{50} of kallikrein from patients with HAE is a better indicator of potency because of the lack of purified enzymes in vivo. Berotralstat inhibited kallikrein-dependent production of bradykinin (half-maximal effective concentration [EC_{50}] 5.56\pm 1.2nM, Study BR-7353-004). Berotralstat has little activity against other serine proteases—the IC_{50} values for other serine proteases were at least 4500-fold higher than that for kallikrein (Study BR-7353-002).
L133: ## 5.3.2. Secondary Pharmacology
L134: Berotralstat at very high concentrations caused an increase in coagulation time in vitro. The concentration required to produce a doubling of the prothrombin time was about 80\mu M in monkey and pig blood, and greater than 100\mu M in blood from other species. The prothrombin time prolongation at the highest concentration tested (100\mu M) was 30%, 67%, 68%, 80%, 86%, 123%, and 144% in dog, rabbit, rat, human, mouse, pig, and monkey blood, respectively.
L135: A 9-month oral toxicity study in monkeys showed that berotralstat at \ge 55 mg/kg/day decreased the blood fibrinogen concentration by approximately 25% starting from week 4 (Study 806-162). No such finding was obtained for rats.
L136: ## 5.3.3. Safety Pharmacology
L137: A full battery of in vitro and in vivo safety pharmacology studies of berotralstat was completed. The in vitro studies evaluated the effects of berotralstat on membrane channel conductance and action potential. In ion channel conductance assays, berotralstat inhibited the currents of human ether à-go-go-related gene potassium channels expressed in human embryonic kidney 293 cells, voltage-gated sodium channels (Nav1.5) in rabbit cardiac Purkinje fibers, and human voltage-gated calcium channels (hCav1.2) expressed in Chinese hamster ovary cells (IC_{50} 0.29, 4.79, and 1.29\muM, respectively; Table 2). Berotralstat also increased the field potential duration and decreased the sodium-spike amplitude in human cell-derived cardiomyocytes (Report 150127.DCS).

No treatment-related effects were observed in the central nervous system and respiratory system. Berotralstat at \ge 50 mg/kg caused a slight but nonsignificant increase in the QTc in conscious monkeys (Study 806-135) (Figure 2). Berotralstat at 100 mg/kg/day caused mild QT prolongation on day 3 in a 4-week toxicity study in monkeys (Study 806-132, discussed below).

L158: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L159: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L160: Repeat-dose toxicity studies of berotralstat of durations of up to 6 months in rats and 9 months in monkeys have been completed to evaluate general toxicity. Studies of durations of up to 6 months and 13 weeks in rats and monkeys, respectively, were reviewed previously. See the nonclinical review by Dr. Luqi Pei on July 19, 2017, in IND 135058 (Document Archiving, Reporting, and Regulatory Tracking System [DARRTS] number 4126737).
L161: This review evaluates two 9-month oral toxicity studies of berotralstat in monkeys (Studies 8001292 and 806-162). These studies were completed by two different laboratories at different times and had no overlap in berotralstat doses. Specifically, Studies 8001292 and 806-162 were completed in 2015 and 2016, respectively. The berotralstat doses were 0, 1.0, 2.5, 7.5 and 20 mg/kg/day in Study 8001292 and 0, 30, 55, and 80 mg/kg/day in Study 806-162. The goal of the second study was to identify the target organs of toxicity of berotralstat because the first study showed no treatment-related effects.
L162: ## Study Title/Number: A 39-Week Study of BCX7353 by Oral Gavage in the Cynomolgus Monkey with a 13-Week Recovery Period (#8001292)
L163: - Monkeys treated orally with up to 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 39 weeks did not reveal any significant toxicity.
- Cynomolgus monkeys (4/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 1, 2.5, 7.5, and 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 39 weeks. No treatment-related toxicity was observed in the 1 to 7.5 mg/kg/day groups. Half of the monkeys in the 20 mg/kg/day group showed mild increases (less than three-fold) in the alanine aminotransferase (ALT) level starting from 6 months. One female in the 20 mg/kg/day group showed single-cell necrosis of hepatocytes in the liver, but without corresponding changes in the levels of liver
L164: ## NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094
## ORLADEYO (berotralstat)
L165: enzymes. The 20 mg/kg/day group also showed an around eight-fold increase in the urinary level of di-22:6-bis(monoacylglycerol) phosphate, a marker of phospholipidosis.
L166: - The no observable adverse effect level (NOAEL) of berotralstat was 20 mg/kg/day, corresponding to a C_{max} of 196.5 ng/mL and an area under the curve from time zero to 24 hours (AUC_{0-24}) of 2875 hour\cdotng/mL on day 273.

L177: ## **Study Title/Number: A 9-Month Study of BCX7353 by Oral Gavage in the Cynomolgus Monkey with a 13-Week Recovery Period (#806-162)**
L178: - The target organs of toxicity of berotralstat in monkeys were the kidney, liver, intestine, lung, spleen, lymph nodes, and bone marrow.
- Cynomolgus monkeys were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 30, 55, and 80 mg/kg/day berotralstat for up to 273 days. The mid-dose (MD) and high-dose (HD) groups showed the following microscopic changes: degeneration (kidney, tubular), hyperplasia (kidney, bone marrow, and spleen), hypertrophy (liver, hepatocellular), vacuolation (adrenal, cortical), and foamy macrophages (lung, lymph node, and intestine). The foamy macrophages were caused by phospholipidosis. The low-dose (LD) group showed foamy macrophages in the intestine and lymph nodes. Although the kidney showed the most severe tissue damage (tubular degeneration and hyperplasia), the intestine was the most sensitive organ. The effects of berotralstat were partially reversible.
- The study did not establish a NOAEL for berotralstat.
L179: (b) (4)
L180: Conducting laboratory and location:
L181: GLP compliance: Yes

Species/strain:	Cynomolgus monkeys

L184: ## Observations and Results: Changes from Control
L185: Parameter	Major Findings
Mortality	No treatment-related effects
Clinical signs	The MD and HD groups showed increases in the incidence of emesis. Four HD monkeys (two of each sex; #242, 245, 262, and 263) had a thin appearance. Six HD females (#222, 245, 246, 248, 263, and 264) showed inappetence.
Bodyweight	Both males and females in the HD group showed dose-dependent decreases in bodyweight. The decrease was 22% and 9% in mean bodyweight in week 39 in HD males and females, respectively (see the graphs below). The HD males showed no increase in mean bodyweight during the study.

Ophthalmoscopy	No treatment-related effects
ECG	No treatment-related effects
Hematology	The MD and HD groups showed a decrease (\le 38\%) in fibrinogen concentration starting from week 4. The HD females showed a decrease (\le 13\%) in hemoglobin concentration starting from week 13.
Clinical chemistry	The MD and HD groups showed increases in AST and ALT levels (up to 4\times and 8.7\times, respectively) starting from week 4. The MD and HD females showed a statistically significant decrease (\downarrow 16\%) in albumin level starting from week 13. The HD males showed a statistically significant decrease (\downarrow 31\%) in phosphorus level starting from week 13.
Urinalysis	No treatment-related effects
Gross pathology	The MD and HD groups showed discoloration in the kidney, liver, and lymph nodes. The respective total incidence of discoloration at termination sacrifice in the C, LD, MD, and HD groups (n=8/group, M and F combined) was 0, 0, 3, and 5 in the kidney; and 0, 0, 1, and 7 in the liver.
Organ weights	The MD and HD groups showed increases in organ weights of the liver, kidney, and spleen (82%, 67%, and 139%, respectively). These increases in absolute organ weights were replicated in those relative to bodyweight and brain weight.
L186: Parameter	Major Findings
Histopathology
Adequate battery:
Yes	
Treatment-related findings were observed in the adrenal glands, bone marrow, kidney, liver, lung, lymph nodes, intestine, and spleen. As expected, findings were more pronounced in the main study; less-marked effects were apparent in the interim and recovery periods.
Histological findings can be grouped into the following: degeneration (kidney, tubular), hyperplasia (kidney, bone marrow and spleen), hypertrophy (liver), vacuolation (adrenal), and foamy macrophages (lung, lymph node, and intestine). Although the kidney showed the most severe tissue damage (tubular degeneration and hyperplasia), the intestine was the most sensitive organ. Only the intestine showed foamy macrophages in the LD group. The findings were not readily reversible. Most findings remained apparent after a recovery period of 13 weeks. The foamy macrophages were caused by phospholipidosis. See Section 16.3 for the incidence table.

Special evaluations	
The study measured BMP levels in urine. BMP is an exploratory biomarker of phospholipidosis. There were dose- and treatment duration-dependent increases in the urinary BMP level (see the table below). The respective increases in the urinary BMP level in the LD, MD, and HD groups at week 39 were 21-, 229-, and 271-fold in males and 29-, 91-, and 192-fold in females.

L194: ## 5.5.2. Genetic Toxicology
L195: ## 5.5.2.1. in Vitro Reverse Mutation Assay in Bacterial Cells (Ames)
L196: ## Study Title/Number: BCX7353: Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay (AE02WJ.502ICH.BTL)
L197: Key study findings:
L198: - Berotralstat tested negative in the Ames test.
L199: GLP compliance: Yes
L200: Test system: *Salmonella typhimurium* TA98, TA100, TA1535, and TA1537, and *Escherichia coli* WP2 *uvrA*
L201: Study is valid: Yes. The assay was performed using the plate incorporation method. Berotralstat concentrations of 5.0 to 1500 µg/plate were tested and considered appropriate. Precipitate and toxicity were observed with berotralstat at 1500 µg/plate. The criteria for a positive response
L202: and valid assay were appropriate. The positive controls yielded a significant increase in the number of mutant colonies, as is typical of the assay.
L203: ## 5.5.2.2. In Vitro Assays in Mammalian Cells
L204: **Study Title/Number:** BCX7353: In Vitro Mammalian Chromosome Aberration Assay in Human Peripheral Blood Lymphocytes [AE02WJ.3411CH.BTL]
L205: Key study findings:
L206: - Berotralstat tested negative in the chromosomal aberration assay in vitro.
L207: GLP compliance: Yes
L208: Test system: Human peripheral blood lymphocytes
L209: Study is valid: Yes. The selection of berotralstat concentrations was reasonable. The highest dose analyzed under each treatment condition produced a reduction in mitotic index (50\pm 5\%). The criteria for a positive response and valid assay were appropriate. The positive controls yielded significant increases in the rate of chromosomal aberrations, as is typical of the assay.
L210: ## 5.5.2.3. In Vivo Clastogenicity Assay in Rodent (Micronucleus Assay)
L211: **Study Title/Number:** BCX7353: In Vivo Mammalian Erythrocyte Micronucleus Assay in Rats (AE02WJ.125M0121ICH.BTL)
L212: Key study findings:
L213: - Berotralstat tested negative in the in vivo micronucleus assay in rats.
L214: GLP compliance: Yes
L215: Test system: Sprague-Dawley rats
L216: Study is valid: Yes. The dose selection of BCX7353 was appropriate (0, 125, 250, and 500 mg/kg single dose by oral gavage). The highest dose was half of the lethal dose in the dose-ranging study. The criteria for a positive response and valid assay were appropriate. Bone marrow was extracted and 4000 polychromatic erythrocytes per animal were analyzed for the frequency of micronuclei. Cytotoxicity was assessed by scoring the number of polychromatic erythrocytes and normochromatic erythrocytes in at least the first 500 total erythrocytes for each animal.
L217: ## 5.5.2.4. Other Genetic Toxicity Studies
L218: Three bacterial reverse mutation assays (Studies AE54DM.502ICH.BTL, AE54DL.502ICH.BTL, and AE54DK.502ICH.BTL) were completed to evaluate the mutagenicity of three BCX7353 impurities: [REDACTED](b) (4). Each assay employed the plate incorporation method and used five tester strains: *S. typhimurium* TA98, TA100, TA1535, and TA1537 and *E. coli* WP2 *uvrA*. The results were negative for all assays and tester strains, except for a positive result for [REDACTED](b) (4) in TA98.
L219: Two tests (initial and repeat) were performed to evaluate the effect of [REDACTED](b) (4) on mutant colonies of test strain TA98. The [REDACTED](b) (4) concentrations ranged from 15 to 5000 µg/plate in both tests. Precipitate was observed at 5000 µg/plate. The 5000 µg/plate concentration in both tests led to an at least two-fold increase in revertant count in the presence of S9 activation (Table 4). The increase was also outside of the 95% vehicle control limit. [REDACTED](b) (4) was considered to produce a positive result in the bacterial gene mutation assay under the test conditions. The [REDACTED](b) (4) level in the drug substance and product will be controlled per the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH)-M7 guidance.

L224: ## 5.5.3. Carcinogenicity
L225: The carcinogenicity potential of berotralstat was evaluated in a 2-year traditional bioassay in rats and in a 26-week study in transgenic mice (reports #806-161 and #20088905, respectively). Wistar rats (60/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 3, 8 and 20 mg/kg berotralstat once daily for 104 weeks. Mice (25/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 8, 20, and 50 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 26 weeks. No evidence of tumorigenicity was observed in rats or mice.
L226: The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC) of the Agency evaluated reports of these studies on March 10 and July 10, 2020 (final reports in the original submission and addendum to the rat report submitted by the Applicant in SDN 029, respectively). The Committee concluded that 1) both studies were adequate, and 2) no evidence of carcinogenicity was observed in either species. See the ECAC meeting minutes and the addendum thereto completed by Dr. Robeena Ariz on March 16 and July 23, 2020, and the nonclinical review and an addendum thereto completed by Dr. Luqi Pei on March 24 and July 23, 2020, respectively (DARRTS IDs 4576054, 4580256, 4645569, and 4645674, respectively).
L227: The ECAC twice evaluated the carcinogenicity data of the 2-year rat study. The first evaluation was performed on March 10, 2020. The ECAC concluded that: 1) berotralstat at 20 mg/kg/day for 2 years increased the incidence of hemangiosarcoma in the mesenteric lymph nodes in male rats, and 2) the relevance of the tumor finding in rats to humans is unknown.
L228: The ECAC's conclusion about the carcinogenicity of berotralstat in rats differed from that of the Applicant. The Applicant argued that hemangiosarcoma associated with berotralstat treatment was a species-specific effect. To support their argument, the Applicant assembled a pathology expert working group (EWG) to re-evaluate the microscopic changes in the mesenteric lymph nodes in male and female rats. By consensus, the EWG revised the tumor type and incidence. The Applicant submitted an amendment to the final report on July 9, 2020 (SDN 029), which outlined the EWG's conclusion. The Applicant stated that new data showed that berotralstat
L229: showed no evidence of tumorigenicity in rats. The relevant data from the original and amended reports are presented in Table 5.
L230: **Table 5. Tumor Findings in the Mesenteric Lymph Node in Males: Study 806-161**

L235: The ECAC evaluated the amended report on July 10, 2020 (discussion via email). The Committee agreed with the Applicant that there was no evidence of tumorigenicity associated with berotralstat in male rats.

L247: ## Observations and Results
L248: Parameter	Major Findings
Mortality	There were no deaths in the study.
Clinical signs	The HD group showed audible breathing in both sexes (incidence, three and six male and female rats, respectively).
Body weight	The HD males showed decreases of 5% in bodyweight and 12% in bodyweight gain in comparison to the control. This was associated with a decrease in food consumption. The females showed no treatment-related effects.
Necropsy findings	Mating/fertility index: No treatment-related effects were observed in either sex. Mating index was 100% in both sexes in all groups. Both fertility and fecundity indices ranged between 96% to 100% in both sexes. There were no apparent differences in menstrual cycles and duration in females. There were 24 pregnant rats in each dose group.
Cesarean section data: No treatment-related effects were observed. The mean number of corpora lutea was 15.7 to 16.8. The mean number of implantation sites was 14.2 to 14.9. The mean number of viable embryos was 13.2 to 14.1. The mean preimplantation loss was 6.2% to 11.2%. The mean number of postimplantation sites was 4.1% to 9.4%.
L249: Abbreviation: HD, high dose
L250: ## Toxicokinetics
L251: Blood levels of berotralstat were measured at 4 hours postdosing on treatment day 1 in both sexes, day 63 in males, and GD 7 in females in toxicokinetics animals (1.0 mL blood/time point, n=4) (Table 6). The plasma berotralstat level increased in a dose-related fashion. A significant increase (approximately 2.5-fold) in the blood level between the first and last days of treatment was observed in both sexes. There was no sex difference in the plasma berotralstat level.

L275: ## **Study Title/Number: An EFD Study of BCX7353 by Oral Gavage in Rabbit (9000815)**
L276: ## **Key study findings:**
L277: - Berotralstat was nonteratogenic in New Zealand white rabbits under the conditions tested.
- Pregnant rabbits were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 20, 50, and 100 mg/kg/day berotralstat during the organogenesis period (GD 7 to 19). They were sacrificed on GD 29 for examination of uterine content.
- The HD group showed statistically significant decreases in bodyweight gain and food consumption (p&lt;0.05). No treatment-related effects were observed in any of the fetal developmental parameters evaluated. The HD group showed abortions and early terminations (two rabbits each). These findings were not considered significant because they were associated with significant maternal toxicity.
- Berotralstat can cross the placenta. The fetal blood berotralstat concentration may be up to 11% of that in maternal blood.

L303: - The PPND study in rats showed that berotralstat had no effect on PPND parameters under the test conditions.

The dams in this period also showed statistically significant decreases in food consumption (up to 10%). No treatment-related effects were observed on any of the other parameters evaluated.

L333: ## 5.5.5. Other Toxicology Studies
L334: ## Neutral Red Uptake Phototoxicity Assay of BCX7353 in BALB/c 3T3 Mouse Fibroblasts (Study No. 20088288)
L335: Key study findings:
L336: - Berotralstat had no phototoxic potential in vitro.
L337: (b) (4)
L338: Conducting Laboratory:
L339: GLP: Yes
L340: This test was performed because berotralstat absorbs light at wavelengths of 290 to 700 nm. Phototoxicity potential was measured by assaying the relative reduction in viability of BALB/c 3T3 mouse fibroblasts treated with berotralstat in the presence and absence of ultraviolet radiation (+UVR and -UVR, respectively). The berotralstat concentration ranged from 1.0 to 56.2 µg/mL. The results showed that berotralstat had no phototoxic potential in vitro.
L341: ## 6. Clinical Pharmacology
L342: ## 6.1. Executive Summary
L343: This is an original (505(b)(1)) NDA submitted by the applicant, BioCryst Pharmaceuticals (BioCryst), on December 3, 2019, seeking marketing approval for ORLADEYO (berotralstat), an orally administered plasma kallikrein inhibitor indicated for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE in patients 12 years of age and older. The proposed dosage form is the active pharmaceutical ingredient (API) (b) (4) in a hard gelatin capsule with a product strength of 150 mg berotralstat
L344: (also referred to as BCX7353) per capsule. The proposed dose is 150 mg3 orally once daily (QD) without regards to food. HAE is a serious, potentially life-threatening, genetically determined disease characterized clinically by recurrent episodes of angioedema of the skin, pharynx, larynx, GI tract, genitals, and extremities. Because there are no oral medications recommended as first-line prophylactic treatment of HAE, berotralstat was granted orphan drug designation on November 1, 2017 and fast track designation on August 2, 2018.
L345: The NDA for berotralstat is supported by studies conducted by BioCryst (IND 135058) and includes 11 phase-1 clinical pharmacology studies in healthy subjects and subjects with renal and hepatic impairment, 2 phase-2 studies, and 1 phase-3 study in subjects with HAE. The program was also supported by the results of several in vitro human biomaterial studies. The major findings for this application are summarized in Section 6.2.
L346: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology/Division of Inflammation and Immune Pharmacology and Pharmacometrics have reviewed the clinical pharmacology data submitted in support of NDA 214094 for berotralstat and found them to be acceptable to support approval from a clinical pharmacology perspective.
L347: ## 6.2. Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L348: ## 6.2.1. General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L349: Fourteen clinical studies provide data on the clinical pharmacology and pharmacodynamics (PD) of berotralstat in this NDA submission (Table 11).
L350: **Table 11. Clinical Pharmacology Studies Conducted for Berotralstat**
L351: Phase	Study #	Description (Dose Studied)
Phase 1	101	SAD and MAD in healthy subjects

107	Renal impairment study (200 mg)

L354: The clinical pharmacology findings for berotralstat are summarized in Table 12.
L355: 3 Rounded from 153 mg berotralstat and equivalent to 175 mg berotralstat hydrochloride.
L356: Table 12. Clinical Pharmacology Summary
L357: Type of Parameter	Description
General Information
Berotralstat exposure	GM Cmax (ng/mL) and %CV	GM AUC (ng.h/mL) and %CV
Steady state, 110 mg, Study 101	97.8 (35%)	1600 (41%)
Steady state, 150 mg, Study 106	158 (24%)	2770 (24%)
Dose proportionality	The AUCtau, Cmax, and Ctau of berotralstat showed a greater than dose-proportional increase in exposure over the 55 to 300 mg (65 to 350 mg [SN]) daily dose range in subjects with HAE. The exposure increase between 110 and 150 mg doses is around two-fold after multiple doses.
Accumulation	Berotralstat accumulates about five-fold when dosed to steady-state. Berotralstat reaches steady-state within 8 to 12 days.
Absorption
Tmax median (range)	The median Tmax of berotralstat when administered with food is 5 hours (range 1 to 8 hours).
Enterohepatic recirculation	Berotralstat likely undergoes enterohepatic recirculation. Multiple plasma concentration peaks were observed over the dosing interval after single- and multiple-dose administration; the second peak typically occurred after food intake.
Effect of food	No differences in the Cmax and AUC of berotralstat were observed following administration with a high-fat meal; however, the median Tmax was delayed 3 hours (median Tmax 2 hours under fasting conditions compared to 5 hours after a high-fat meal).
Distribution
Plasma protein binding	Plasma protein binding is approximately 99%.
Elimination
Half-life	Median (range) elimination t1/2 is 93 hours (39 to 152 hours). Effective half-life is 89 hours, consistent with the elimination half-life.
Primary metabolic pathways	In human hepatocytes and S9 fractions, approximately 10% loss of berotralstat was observed over an extended incubation period. In vitro incubations with recombinant CYP3A4 and CYP2D6 resulted in 15% and 22% loss of BCX7353, respectively, with approximately 10% loss in incubations without the cofactor NADPH.
Following a single dose of 300 mg [14C]-BCX7353, unchanged berotralstat accounted for 34% of plasma radioactivity. Eight metabolites were detected, each accounted for <8% of the radioactivity. In vitro and in vivo metabolic pathways include oxidation, oxidative deamination, hydrogenation, N-dealkylation, N-acetylation, and N-carbamoylation.
Excretion
Primary excretion pathways	After a single dose of 300 mg [14C]-BCX7353, approximately 85.5% of the radioactivity administered was excreted over a 50-day collection period; the predominant component in urine and feces was unchanged berotralstat, but metabolites were also identified in both urine and feces. Overall, 79.4% and 9.2% of the administered dose was excreted in feces and urine, respectively.
Intrinsic Factors
Renal impairment	The AUC and Cmax of berotralstat were increased by approximately 13 and 47%, respectively, in subjects with severe renal impairment compared to healthy subjects. This change in exposure does not require dose adjustment of berotralstat.
L358: Type of Parameter	Description
Hepatic impairment	
There was no change in berotralstat exposure in subjects with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A) compared to healthy subjects. The AUC and C_{max} were increased by ~80%, in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B), whereas the C_{max} increase in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh C) was 27% (there was no AUC increase), in comparison to healthy subjects. Although the exposure increase in subjects with severe hepatic impairment was less than that in those with moderate impairment, there was a 2.6-fold decrease in protein binding in this subpopulation. Additionally, there was a 50% increase in the berotralstat t_{1/2} in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. In patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B or C, respectively), the recommended dose of berotralstat is 110 mg QD.

Weight	
Slightly higher exposure of berotralstat (~15%) was predicted in adults of 40 to 60 kg bodyweight and adolescents 12 to 18 years of age, compared to adults of 60 to 80 kg bodyweight. This increase is not considered sufficiently clinically meaningful to warrant dose adjustment in this subpopulation. See the pharmacometrics review by Dr. Jing Niu in Section 16.3.2 for further details.

Extrinsic Factors and Drug Interactions
PK of berotralstat affected	
Berotralstat is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP. The berotralstat C_{max} and AUC were increased by 25% and 69%, respectively, with concomitant administration of cyclosporine, a P-gp and BCRP inhibitor. Dose adjustment (110 mg QD) is recommended when berotralstat is administered with P-gp and/or BCRP inhibitors; this will be communicated to the Applicant during labeling interactions. Additionally, P-gp inducers (e.g., rifampin, St. John's wort) may decrease berotralstat plasma concentration, leading to reduced efficacy of berotralstat. Use of berotralstat with P-gp inducers is not recommended.

Effect of berotralstat on the PK of other drugs	
Berotralstat 150 mg QD is a moderate inhibitor of CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 and a weak inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP2C19. Berotralstat at 300 mg is an inhibitor of P-gp but not of BCRP. Appropriate monitoring and dose titration are recommended for concomitant medications with a narrow therapeutic index that are predominantly metabolized by CYP2D6 (e.g., thioridazine, pimozide) or CYP3A4 (e.g., cyclosporine, fentanyl) or are sensitive P-gp substrates.
CYP2C9 substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 150 mg QD, the C_{max} and AUC of tolbutamide were increased by 19% and 73%, respectively.
CYP2C19 substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 150 mg QD, the C_{max} and AUC of omeprazole were increased by 21% and 24%, respectively.
CYP3A4 substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 150 mg QD, the C_{max} and AUC of midazolam were increased by 45% and 124%, respectively; the C_{max} and AUC of amlodipine were increased by 45% and 77%, respectively; and the C_{max} and AUC of danazol were decreased by approximately 20%.
CYP2D6 substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 150 mg QD, the C_{max} and AUC of dextromethorphan were increased by 196% and 178%, respectively, and the C_{max} and AUC of desipramine were increased by 64% and 87%, respectively.
P-gp substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 300 mg QD, the C_{max} and AUC of digoxin were increased by 58% and 48%, respectively.
BCRP substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 300 mg QD, the C_{max} and AUC of rosuvastatin were decreased by approximately 20%.
L359: Type of Parameter	Description
Pharmacologic Effects
Kallikrein inhibition	Kallikrein inhibition was evaluated in clinical studies using an ex vivo assay of amidolytic cleavage of a kallikrein-specific substrate in pre- and post-dose samples. E-R analyses demonstrated a high correlation between ex vivo kallikrein inhibition activity and the plasma concentration of berotralstat. The berotralstat concentration and kallikrein inhibition relationship in subjects with HAE was well described by a sigmoidal E_{max} model with a predicted EC_{50} of 11 ng/mL. See the pharmacometrics review by Dr. Jing Niu in Section 16.3.2 for further details.
L360: Source: Module 2.7.2, Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Studies, page 16 (modified by the reviewer)

L362: ## 6.3. Summary of the Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L363: The key issues identified during the clinical pharmacology review are listed in Table 13.
L364: **Table 13. Key Issues Identified in the Review Process**
L365: Review Issue	Recommendations and Comments
Efficacy and safety	The pivotal randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 study (302) evaluated two doses (110 mg QD1 and 150 mg QD) for a 24-week treatment duration. The study met its primary endpoint for both dose levels, with the 110 and 150 mg berotralstat doses reducing HAE attacks by 30% (p=0.024) and 44% (p<0.001), respectively, compared with placebo.
Key safety issues noted during the review were GI adverse events of diarrhea, nausea or vomiting, and abdominal pain; transient benign maculopapular rash; and asymptomatic transaminase elevations. In addition, at a dose of 450 mg (i.e., three-fold the 150 mg dose), berotralstat showed a mean QTc increase of 15.9 ms (90% upper CI bound of 23.5 ms); the observed increase in QTcF was concentration-dependent. Refer to the clinical and QT-IRT reviews by Dr. Shaz Siddiqui and Dr. Girish Bende, respectively, for more details.
General dosing instructions	The Applicant recommends a dose of 150 mg QD for the proposed indication. However, based on the clinical assessment of benefit/risk, the review team recommends a starting dose of 150 mg QD, with an option of reducing the dose to 110 mg QD if GI-related adverse events persist.
The Applicant proposed to dose berotralstat without regards to food. Although food had no significant impact on the berotralstat PK, the Applicant administered berotralstat with food in the phase 3 study to avoid GI-related adverse events. A recommendation to administer berotralstat with food will be reflected in the product labeling.
L366: Review Issue	Recommendations and Comments
Dosing in patient subgroups (intrinsic factors)	
In a dedicated single-dose hepatic impairment study (108), the berotralstat AUC and C_{max} were increased by ~80%, in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B), whereas the C_{max} increase in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh C) subjects was 27% (there was no AUC increase) in comparison to healthy subjects. While the exposure increase in subjects with severe hepatic impairment was less than that in those with moderate impairment, there was a 2.6-fold decrease in protein binding in this subpopulation. Additionally, there was a 50% increase in the berotralstat terminal half-life in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. At the time of NDA submission, the Applicant did not propose dose adjustment in subjects with moderate and severe hepatic impairment. However, during the review cycle, the FDA raised concern regarding the concentration-dependent QTc prolongation potential at high berotralstat exposures in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. Thereafter, the Applicant proposed a reduced starting dose of 110 mg QD in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class B or C). See Section 6.3.2 for further details. No dose adjustment is recommended in patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class A) or renal impairment.

Dosing in patient subgroups (extrinsic factors)	
Berotralstat is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP. Cyclosporine, a P-gp and BCRP inhibitor, increased the C_{max} of berotralstat by 25% and the AUC_{inf} by 69%. The Applicant did not propose dose adjustment for this drug interaction. However, a recommendation for dose adjustment (110 mg QD) for berotralstat with concomitant administration of P-gp and/or BCRP inhibitors will be reflected in the product labeling. Additionally, the Applicant did not address the impact of P-gp inducers on the exposure of berotralstat. The use of P-gp inducers with berotralstat is not recommended. See Section 6.3.2 for further details. Berotralstat is a moderate inhibitor of CYP2D6, CYP3A4, and CYP2C9 and a weak inhibitor of CYP2C19. It is also an inhibitor of P-gp. Appropriate monitoring and dose titration are recommended for concomitant medications with sensitive substrates and substrates with a narrow therapeutic index that are predominantly metabolized by CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 and are sensitive P-gp substrates.

Labeling	Recommendations reflected in final labeling.
Bridge between the to-be-marketed and clinical trial formulations	
For the initial phase 1 and 2 studies (Studies 101, 102, 103, 105, and 203) API in hydroxypropyl methylcellulose capsules were used. The hydroxypropyl methylcellulose capsules and phase 3 formulation (b) (4)-capsule were bridged in Study 103. Subsequently, Study 113 was conducted to demonstrate the bioequivalence of the formulation used in the phase 3 study (two capsules per dose) and the commercial formulation (one capsule per dose).
L367: Source: Reviewer's summary of clinical pharmacology data submitted in NDA 214094.

L369: Abbreviations: API, active pharmaceutical ingredient; AUC_{inf}, area under the curve to infinity; BCRP, breast cancer resistance protein; CI, confidence interval; C_{max}, maximum concentration; CYP, cytochrome P450; GI, gastrointestinal; HAE, hereditary angioedema; NDA, new drug application; P-gp, P-glycoprotein; PK, pharmacokinetics; QD, four times daily; QTc, corrected QT; QTcF, Fridericia-corrected QT; QT-IRT, QT-Interdisciplinary Review Team
L370: ## 6.3.1. Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L371: ## 6.3.1.1. Pharmacokinetics
L372: - The PK of berotralstat in patients with HAE is similar to that of healthy subjects.
- Berotralstat exposure (C_{max} and AUC) increases greater than proportionally with dose, and steady state is reached within 8 to 12 days. Accumulation is around five-fold at the 110 and 150 mg doses.
- The median time to the C_{max} (T_{max}) of berotralstat when administered with food is 5 hours (range 1 to 8 hours). No differences in the C_{max} and AUC of berotralstat were
L373: observed following administration with a high-fat meal; however, the median T_{max} was delayed by 3 hours, from 2 hours (fasted) to 5 hours (fed).
L374: - Plasma protein binding is approximately 99%. After a single dose of radiolabeled berotralstat 300 mg, the blood-to-plasma ratio was approximately 0.92.
- The median elimination half-life of berotralstat was ~93 hours (range 39 to 152 hours).
- Berotralstat is metabolized by cytochrome P450 (CYP)2D6 and CYP3A4 with low turnover in vitro. After a single oral radiolabeled berotralstat 300 mg dose, unchanged berotralstat represented 34% of the total plasma radioactivity, with each of the eight metabolites accounting for 1.8 to 7.8% of the total radioactivity.
- After a single oral radiolabeled berotralstat 300 mg dose, approximately 9% was excreted in urine (3.4% unchanged; range 1.8 to 4.7%) and 79% was excreted in feces.
- Among the factors evaluated in the population PK analyses (bodyweight, age, gender and race), only bodyweight was identified as a significant predictor of berotralstat clearance and volume of distribution; higher exposure (~15%) was predicted in 40 to 60 kg adults and adolescents 12 to 18 years of age, compared to 60 to 80 kg adults.
L375: ## 6.3.1.2. Pharmacodynamics
L376: Primary and secondary nonclinical PD studies showed that berotralstat is a specific inhibitor of human plasma kallikrein activity. Concentration-dependent inhibition of plasma kallikrein, measured as a reduction from baseline of specific enzyme activity, was demonstrated after oral administration of berotralstat to patients with HAE in the clinical development program. The EC_{50} of kallikrein inhibition measured from a pool of subjects with HAE was 11 ng/mL. A 110 mg dose provided a mean C_{tau} of 4.7-fold the EC_{50} (Study 203).
L377: ## Cardiac Electrophysiology
L378: The effect of berotralstat was evaluated in thorough QT Study (106), a phase 1, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group, multiple-dose study in healthy subjects. At the therapeutic dose of 150 mg QD, berotralstat does not prolong the Fridericia-corrected QT (QTcF) interval to a clinically relevant extent. A positive QT prolongation was observed at a dose of 450 mg (QD for 14 days) (Table 14). Refer to the QT-IRT review by Dr. Girish Bende for more details.
L379: **Table 14. Point Estimates and 90% CIs (FDA Analysis) From TQT Study 106.**

L383: ## 6.3.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L384: ## 6.3.2.1. General Dosing
L385: The Applicant's proposed dose of berotralstat is one 150 mg capsule taken orally QD without regard to food. Although the numerically greater treatment effect in the high-dose arm supports the recommended dose of 150 mg QD, the totality of the efficacy and safety data support the option for dose reduction to 110 mg QD if GI adverse events (AEs) are observed with the 150 mg QD dose. Refer to the clinical review by Dr. Shaz Siddiqui for more details (Section 8). The applicant proposed to dose berotralstat without regard to food. Although food had no major impact on the berotralstat PK, berotralstat was administered with food in the phase 3 study to avoid GI-related AEs. A recommendation to administer berotralstat with food will be reflected in the product labeling.
L386: ## 6.3.2.2. Therapeutic Individualization
L387: - In patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B or C, respectively), the recommended dose of berotralstat is 110 mg QD.
- No adjustment of the starting dose of berotralstat is recommended for patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A).
- No adjustment of the starting dose of berotralstat is recommended for patients with renal impairment.
- Berotralstat is a substrate for P-glycoprotein (P-gp) and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) and dose adjustment (110 mg QD) is recommended in cases of concomitant administration with P-gp and/or BCRP inhibitors. This recommendation will be reflected in the product labeling. The use of P-gp inducers with berotralstat is not recommended.
L388: ## 6.3.2.3. Outstanding Issues
L389: None.
L390: ## 6.3.3. Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L391: ## Does the Clinical Pharmacology Program Provide Supportive Evidence of Effectiveness?
L392: The efficacy of berotralstat for the prevention of HAE attacks in patients 12 years of age and older with type I or II HAE was demonstrated in Part 1 of the multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group Study 302. Adult and adolescent patients who experienced at least two investigator-confirmed attacks within the first 8 weeks of the run-in period were randomized at a 1:1:1 ratio (berotralstat 110 mg, berotralstat 150 mg, or placebo by oral administration once daily, with food) for the 24-week treatment period (Part 1); 41 subjects were treated with berotralstat 110 mg QD, 40 subjects with berotralstat 150 mg QD, and 40 subjects were treated with placebo QD. Patients discontinued other prophylactic HAE medications prior to entering the study; however, all patients were allowed to use rescue medications for treatment of breakthrough HAE attacks. A history of laryngeal angioedema attacks was reported in 74% of the patients and 75% reported prior use of long-term prophylaxis. The median attack rate during the prospective run-in period (baseline attack rate)
L393: was 2.9/month. Seventy percent of patients enrolled had a baseline attack rate of \ge 2 attacks/month. Parts 2 and 3 of the study are ongoing. A schematic is shown in Figure 3.
L394: **Figure 3. Schematic of Study 302**
L395: Source: Study 302 protocol, page 48
L396: Abbreviation: QD, once daily
L397: The primary efficacy endpoint was the rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during dosing throughout the 24-week treatment period (Part 1). The study met its primary endpoint for both dose levels, with the 110 and 150 mg berotralstat doses reducing HAE attacks by 30% (p=0.024) and 44% (p&lt;0.001), respectively, compared with placebo. The negative binomial analysis-estimated attack rates per 28 days were 1.65 for subjects who received berotralstat 110 mg, 1.31 for those administered berotralstat 150 mg, and 2.35 for subjects who received placebo over the 24-week Part 1 dosing period. The reductions in attack rates are shown in Figure 4. Note that a total of six adolescents was enrolled in the study, which was insufficient for adequate interpretation in the subpopulation; however, efficacy may be extrapolated from adult to adolescent patients given that the pathophysiology of the disease is the same in both populations. Refer to Section 8 for further details.
L398: In Study 302, in the 174 samples (collected with no particular relationship to the timing of study drug administration), the mean (standard deviation) percentage inhibition of plasma kallikrein averaged across postbaseline visits was 62% (\pm 15.0\%), 68% (\pm 33\%), and -21\% (\pm 56\%) for the berotralstat 110 mg QD, berotralstat 150 mg QD, and placebo groups, respectively.

L401: ## Is the Proposed Dosing Regimen Appropriate for the General Patient Population for Which the Indication is Being Sought?
L402: Yes, the proposed dosing regimen is reasonable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The basis for the dose selection and findings from phase 2 Study 203 is discussed below.
L403: Study 203 was a phase 2, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, four-part, parallel-group, dose-response study that evaluated the safety, tolerability, PK, PD, and efficacy of berotralstat in subjects with HAE over a 28-day dosing period; the doses evaluated were 55 mg QD to 300 mg QD. The primary efficacy endpoint was the number of confirmed HAE attacks. A statistical comparison of confirmed attacks per week is shown in Table 15. Treatment with berotralstat at doses \ge 110 mg for 28 days resulted in a reduction in the rate of angioedema attacks compared with placebo in subjects with type I or type II HAE (p \le 0.006), with a 70% reduction in attack rate at a berotralstat dose of 110 mg representing a clinically meaningful treatment effect. No further increase in efficacy at doses above 110 mg was observed. The Applicant hypothesized that the efficacy of the 218 and 300 mg doses was probably masked by GI AEs that may have been misattributed as early symptoms of abdominal angioedema attacks.
L404: Table 15. Statistical Analysis of the Overall Confirmed Attack Rate: Study BCX7353-203

Berotralstat 218 mg	14	0.467	0.117	-0.446	-48.865	-0.742, -0.150	0.003
Berotralstat 110 mg	14	0.271	0.117	-0.642	-70.327	-0.938, -0.346	<0.001

L406: Source: Study 203 Clinical Study Report, page 95 (dihydrochloride salt converted to free base), with modifications
L407: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; LSM, least-squares mean; SE, standard error
L408: Although there was no apparent correlation between the confirmed attack rate and berotralstat plasma exposure over the dose range studied (55 to 300 mg), the steady-state berotralstat area under the curve over the dosing interval (AUC_{\tau}) and maximum plasma concentration over the dosing interval (C_{\tau}) were correlated with the confirmed attack rate at the lower dose range of 55 to 110 mg. Refer to the pharmacometrics review by Dr. Jing Niu (Section 16.3.2) for further details. In addition, the results suggested that maintaining a threshold concentration of at least four-fold the EC_{50} for plasma kallikrein inhibition will result in a clinically meaningful reduction in attack rate. The proportions of subjects with an average berotralstat plasma C_{\tau} greater than four-fold the EC_{50} were 0/7, 8/14, 14/14, and 16/16 in the 55, 110, 218, and 300 mg dose groups, respectively. A 110 mg QD dose provided a mean C_{\tau} of 4.7-fold the EC_{50}, with a corresponding reduction in attack rate of 70% (p&lt;0.001) versus placebo, and a 55 mg QD dose provided a mean C_{\tau} of 1.5-fold the EC_{50}, with no change in attack rate versus placebo (p=0.811). Hence, 110 mg QD berotralstat was selected for confirmation of efficacy in the phase 3 study. The higher doses (218 mg and 300 mg QD) were not evaluated in the phase 3 study because of the GI AEs observed in Study 203. The Applicant noted that berotralstat has nonlinear PK, and a two-fold increase in steady-state exposure was observed at 150 mg as compared to 110 mg. Therefore, to evaluate the dose-response between 110 mg and 218 mg, 150 mg QD was selected as the second dose in the phase 3 study. Note that berotralstat has a long half-life of ~93 hours and as such a QD dosing regimen was considered reasonable. Other dosing regimens were not evaluated in the clinical development plan.
L409: ## Is an Alternative Dosing Regimen or Management Strategy Required for Subpopulations Based on Intrinsic Patient Factors?
L410: Yes, in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B or C), a lower 110 mg QD dose of berotralstat is recommended. No dose modification is recommended in patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A) or renal impairment. Additionally, there is no need for dose adjustment based on age or bodyweight. See below for further details and the basis of these recommendations.
L411: ## Patients with Hepatic Impairment
L412: The impact of hepatic impairment on the berotralstat PK was assessed in a single-dose, open-label study (Study 108) in adult male and female subjects with normal hepatic function and varying degrees of hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A, B, or C). Six subjects in each group received a single oral dose of 150 mg berotralstat.
L413: The PK results are shown in Table 16. Exposure of berotralstat increased in subjects with hepatic impairment, but the increase in exposure in subjects with severe hepatic impairment was lower than that in those with moderate hepatic impairment. The berotralstat unbound fraction was similar in subjects with mild hepatic impairment compared with matched healthy subjects with normal hepatic function (1.2), and the median unbound fraction was approximately 1.5 and 2.6 in subjects with moderate and severe hepatic impairment, respectively. The Applicant noted that there was no physiological basis for moderate hepatic disease to have a greater impact on berotralstat disposition than severe disease, and so proposed to pool the moderate and severe hepatic impairment groups for comparison with healthy matched controls (Table 16). However, the reviewer did not agree with this approach because decreased protein binding may impact the pharmacologically active unbound drug in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment.

L418: Furthermore, based on the pooled PK approach, the Applicant proposed no dose adjustment in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment because higher doses of up to 300 mg QD berotralstat were considered to be safe and generally well-tolerated in subjects with HAE (Study 203). However, the following issues were noted by the reviewer:
L419: - Although a higher dose of 300 mg QD was evaluated in the phase 2 Study 203 and concluded to be generally safe, the study duration was relatively short (28 days) and the number of subjects exposed to 300 mg QD was small (N=18), reducing the level of support for higher exposure of berotralstat.
- The berotralstat half-life was increased by ~50% in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. Berotralstat accumulates by around five-fold when dosed to steady-state. The accumulation of berotralstat in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment is expected to be elevated because of the longer half-life.
- There was a 1.5- and 2-fold decrease in protein binding in subjects with moderate and severe hepatic impairment, respectively, and so the pharmacologically active unbound drug concentration would be higher in these patients.
L420: ## NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094
## ORLADEYO (berotralstat)
L421: - At a 450 mg QD dose of berotralstat (C_{max,ss} 577 ng/mL), the estimated \Delta\Delta QTcF was 15.9 ms with a two-sided 90% upper bound of 23.5 ms.
L422: Because berotralstat is proposed for chronic use and associated with a potential QTc risk, an information request (IR) was sent to the Applicant (dated June 26, 2020) to consider alternate dosing recommendations in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. In response to the IR, the applicant simulated the following dosing regimens in subjects with HAE using population PK Model 258 (BCX7353-PPK-01-A2):
L423: - Alternate daily dose of 110 mg QD
- Alternate dosing regimen 150 mg every other day
L424: Based on the simulation results and patient compliance considerations, the Applicant proposed a reduced dose of 110 mg QD berotralstat in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. This proposed dosing recommendation for subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment is acceptable.
L425: ## Patients with Renal Impairment
L426: The impact of renal impairment on the berotralstat PK was assessed in a single-dose, open-label study (Study 107) in adult male and female subjects with severe renal impairment and matched healthy subjects with normal renal function. Seven subjects in the following two groups received a single oral dose of 200 mg berotralstat.
L427: - Group A: normal renal function (creatinine clearance [CL_{CR}] \ge 90 mL/min)
- Group B: severe renal impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] &lt; 30 mL/min/1.73 m^2; not yet on dialysis)
L428: Compared to matched healthy subjects, the berotralstat C_{max} was approximately 39% higher in subjects with severe renal impairment following single-dose administration of 200 mg berotralstat; however, the AUC was similar in the two groups. Note that healthy subjects and those with severe renal impairment were enrolled using different criteria—CL_{CR} and eGFR, respectively. During the NDA review, the Applicant was informed (IR of June 30, 2020) that only one criterion; i.e., CL_{CR} or eGFR, should be used for comparison of PK between the two groups. The Applicant reanalyzed the study data using CL_{CR} as the criterion. For classification of subjects by CL_{CR}, the applicant used the CL_{CR} value, either at screening or baseline, that the respective site had used for classification by MDRD during the study. One subject (b) (6) in the severe renal impairment group had a screening and baseline CL_{CR} of 38 mL/min and was excluded from the severe renal impairment group (CL_{CR} &lt; 30 L/min). The updated statistical comparison of the berotralstat PK between subjects with normal and severe renal impairment using the CL_{CR} criterion was provided by the Applicant on July 10, 2020 (Table 17). The C_{max} and AUC were increased by approximately 47% and 13%, respectively, in subjects with severe renal impairment compared to healthy subjects using the CL_{CR} criterion. The half-life and protein binding were similar in subjects with severe renal impairment and healthy subjects.
