L834: ## Additional Safety Assessments from Study 302, Part 2
L835: In addition to the 6 month placebo-controlled safety data from Part 1, the NDA submission included interim safety data from 4 months of Study 302, Part 2. No new safety signals were identified during the review of Part 2 interim data. Similar to Part 1, GI adverse reactions such as abdominal pain, vomiting and diarrhea were common and occurred more frequently with the higher 150 mg dose than the lower 110 mg dose. No significant elevations in transaminases or bilirubin were reported. There were 31 study drug discontinuations, mostly occurring due to subject withdrawal for reasons unrelated to study drug or perceived lack of efficacy. Four subjects in the 150 mg group discontinued due to an adverse event (palpitations/tachycardia, abdominal pain, abdominal pain/diarrhea, and anal incontinence). One subject in the 110 mg group discontinued due to nausea. There were two SAEs: obesity in the 110 mg group and a medical observation in the 150 mg group. The medical observation SAE was due to hospitalization for a routine thyroid cancer screening procedure using radiolabeled iodine in a subject with a history of treated thyroid papillary carcinoma and hypothyroidism. Neither event appeared related to the study drug. No AESIs occurred in Part 2 of Study 302.
L836: ## Vital Signs
L837: Overall, no clinically meaningful changes within or between treatment groups were observed for pulse rate, respiratory rate, or mean blood pressure.
L838: ## Electrocardiograms
L839: Among all treatment groups, one subject in the placebo group had a clinically significant abnormal ECG finding. This subject developed sinus bradycardia with first degree atrioventricular block, which was reported as an AE. The Applicant conducted a thorough QT study, which is summarized below.
L840: ## QT
L841: A thorough QT study (Study BCX7353-106) was conducted in healthy volunteers evaluating berotralstat doses of 150 mg and 450 mg QD for 14 days and reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team (IRT) for Cardiac Safety Studies. Berotralstat caused a dose and concentration-dependent increase in the QT interval over the range of doses evaluated; however, at the peak concentrations with the recommended dose of 150 mg QD, the QT interval was not prolonged to any clinically relevant extent at Day 14. At a dose of 450 mg QD (Day 14), exposures were ~3-fold higher than achieved at the recommended 150 mg dose, and the mean QTc increased significantly (refer to the IRT review by Dr. Girish Bende and Clinical Pharmacology review in this document for details).
L842: *Reviewer comment: Because the QT prolongation observed with berotralstat is dose and concentration dependent and berotralstat exhibits nonlinear PK, administering even twice the recommended dose carries a potential risk of clinically significant QTc prolongation. Of note, this safety issue has been discussed with the Applicant during previous interactions for the acute*
L843: *HAE attack development program which proposes to administer higher doses of berotralstat. In light of an ongoing development program for acute attacks, Sections 1 (Limitation of Use statement), 2 (Dosage and Administration), 5 (Warnings and Precautions) and 12 (Pharmacodynamics and Pharmacokinetics) of the PI and the PPI will be revised to ensure that prescribers are aware of this potential risk and patients do not take higher or additional doses.*
L844: ## Immunogenicity
L845: Immunogenicity studies were not required or performed as part of this study.
L846: ## 8.3.5. Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L847: Rash was designated *a priori* as an adverse event of special interest. Reports of skin disorders were rare and occurred as single events: pruritis, acne, dry skin, alopecia, petechiae, purpura and echymosis. One subject treated with 150 mg berotralstat experienced mild petechiae, which was assessed to be caused by mechanical irritation. The purpuric rash was discussed under treatment discontinuations due to TEAEs, but was not felt to be related to study drug.
L848: Although gastrointestinal adverse events were common and a few cases of transaminase elevation occurred, no hepatobiliary adverse events were reported with berotralstat treatment in Part 1 of Study 302.
L849: ## 8.3.6. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
L850: The OCS Analysis Toolbox DM Tool and OCS Analysis studio risk assessment tools were used to analyze safety in Study 302 by the following demographic subgroups: sex, age, race, ethnicity, and region. This review did not identify a meaningful difference in overall TEAEs between berotralstat and placebo within any subgroup. However, the relatively low frequency of other SAEs and adverse dropouts in addition to the small number of subjects within certain subgroups made it difficult to draw any further conclusions regarding an imbalance in safety based on demographic or disease characteristics. Study 302 only enrolled six adolescent subjects. Because the Applicant is seeking an indication for patients 12 years of age and older, safety signals in the 12 to 17-year age group were analyzed in more detail (Table 47).

L854: Abbreviations: TEAE, treatment-emergent adverse event; USUBJID, Unique Subject ID; SAE, serious adverse event; F, female; M, male; N, no
L855: The majority of adolescent subjects were white similar to the general safety population. Although small in number, there was adequate representation across the age spectrum with half of the subjects being 12 years of age. There were no SAEs or TEAEs leading to treatment discontinuation. Infections and GI disorders were the most common TEAEs, all of which were grade 1 or 2 severity. The types of infection were typical for this age group. Despite the small numbers, there was no apparent difference in the safety profile of berotralstat in adolescent subjects compared to adults that would raise concern or alter the benefit-risk assessment in this age group.
L856: ## 8.3.7. Specific Safety Studies: BCX7353-204
L857: ## Trial Design
L858: This is an ongoing two-arm open-label phase 2 study designed to evaluate the long-term safety of prophylactic treatment with daily oral berotralstat at two dose levels in subjects with HAE. The study is also evaluating the long-term effectiveness and impact on QoL or general well-being of subjects who receive berotralstat prophylactic treatment, and the subject's satisfaction with the medication. Target enrollment is 475 subjects with approximately 250 subjects from the United States. The study population includes adults (18 years and older) and adolescents (12 to 17 years of age) with a confirmed diagnosis of type I or II HAE who are either naïve or have received berotralstat in another study. Both 110 mg QD and 150 mg QD doses of berotralstat were initially being evaluated; however, following the completion of Study 302 Part 1, all subjects are receiving berotralstat 150 mg QD.
L859: Screening visits are not required for previous berotralstat study participants if the last dose of study drug was &lt;84 days prior. Subjects rolling over from another berotralstat study without treatment interruption or with &lt;14 days of study drug interruption could have final study visit assessments for the parent study and baseline assessments for Study 204 performed at the same visit. The same values may be used for both assessments when possible.
L860: ## NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094
## ORLADEYO (berotralstat)
L861: On-treatment study visits occur at weeks 2, 4, 8, 12 and every 12 weeks thereafter, for a study duration of up to 240 weeks (approximately 5 years), until another mechanism becomes available to provide the drug to the subject (e.g., market access), or until the Applicant discontinues development of the product for the prevention of angioedema attacks, whichever occurs first. Telephone contact will occur at weeks 16, 20, 28, 32, 40, 44, 52, 56, 64, 68, 76, 80, 88, and 92.
L862: A schematic of the study is provided in Figure 9 and a schedule of assessments in Table 48.

L871: ## NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094
L872: ## ORLADEYO (berotralstat)
L873: Abbreviations: ACR = spot urine microalbumin to creatinine ratio; AE = adverse event; AE-QoL = Angioedema Quality of Life questionnaire; ALP = alkaline phosphatase; ALT = alanine aminotransferase; AST = aspartate aminotransferase; BMI = body mass index; C1-INH = C1 esterase inhibitor; BMI = body mass index; C3 = complement 3; C4 = complement 4; CK-MB = creatine kinase MB isoenzyme; CRF = case report form; ECG = electrocardiogram; EOS = end of study; FSH = follicle stimulating hormone; GGT = gamma glutamyl transferase; HAE = hereditary angioedema; HBV = hepatitis B virus; HCV = hepatitis C virus; HIV = human immunodeficiency virus; HLA = human leukocyte antigen; IXRS = interactive (voice/web) response system; LLN = lower limit of normal; NGAL = neutrophil gelatinase-associated lipocalin; PG = pharmacogenomic; PK = pharmacokinetic; QoL = quality of life; QTcF = QT interval corrected using Fridericia's method; TSQM = Treatment Satisfaction Questionnaire for Medication.
L874: - The baseline visit should be within 28 days of the screening visit. Subjects who have interrupted study drug from a previous BCX7353 study for &lt; 84 days are not required to undergo a screening visit. For subjects directly rolling over from another BCX7353 study without treatment interruption or subjects who interrupt study drug for &lt; 14 days, the final on-treatment visit in the prior BCX7353 study will be considered the baseline visit for this study.
- The Week 2 visit will consist of monitoring liver function tests only (ALT, AST, GGT, total and direct bilirubin, ALP); urine and additional tubes of blood may be required to accommodate reflex testing for abnormal GGT (see Table 4). If preferred by the subject and clinical site, laboratory values may be drawn and resulted locally, with results entered into the CRF.
- Informed consent will be signed at the screening visit for subjects requiring a screening visit and will be signed for all other subjects at the baseline visit or final on treatment visit in the parent study (which serves as the subject's baseline visit in this study).
- After the Week 12 visit, clinic staff must contact the subjects at least once every 4 weeks between clinic visits to assess the subject's overall well-being and any issues with regards to study drug tolerability. During all calls, sites will also discuss study drug compliance and any issues with the diary or study drug.
- Medical history and medication history will be completed at screening (for subjects requiring a screening visit) and at baseline. At the Screening visit (or baseline for those with no screening visit), the HAE Medical and Medication history form will be updated from the previous HAE study, as applicable, with additional questions to be answered if provided.
- BMI calculation and height at screening; weight is to be recorded at each in-clinic visit. Adolescents should also have height recorded at Weeks 48, 96, 144, 192, and 240.
- Please see Table 4 for analytes to be assessed. The lipid panel as part of clinical chemistry will only be measured at baseline, Week 12 and then approximately every 12 weeks.
- Full physical examinations will be performed at screening and baseline; symptom-directed physical examinations will be performed at all post-baseline visits.
- For women of childbearing potential (including adolescents), regardless of contraception or lifestyle, a serum pregnancy test will be administered at screening, urine pregnancy tests will be assessed at all subsequent visits as indicated in the table. Demonstration of a negative urine pregnancy test will be required prior to dispensing study drug.
- To include blood pressure and pulse rate. Temperature and respiratory rate will be captured at screening and baseline only. Prior to obtaining vital signs, subjects should rest in a supine position for at least 5 minutes.
- For a woman who declares that she has been post-menopausal \le 2 years or was considered a woman of childbearing potential in the previous study. The sample may be drawn at baseline for those who do not undergo a screening visit; however, the subject must have a negative urine pregnancy test at baseline prior to receiving study drug.
- For subjects that have not participated in a previous BCX7353 study, a clinical diagnosis of HAE Type I or II, defined as having a C1-INH functional level below 50% and a C4 level below the lower LLN reference range, as assessed during the screening period. In the absence of a low C4 value drawn during the inter-critical period, 1 of the following is acceptable to confirm the diagnosis of HAE assessed during the Screening period: 1) a SERPING-1 gene mutation known or likely to be associated with HAE Type I or II; 2) a confirmed family history of C1-INH deficiency; 3) a C4 redrawn and retested during an attack with the results below the LLN reference range. For subjects with C1-INH function \ge 50% but less than the assay LLN, a SERPING-1 gene mutation known or likely to be associated with HAE Type I or II assessed during the screening period or a repeat C1-INH functional level &lt; 50% will be considered acceptable for enrollment.
- A sample for HLA analysis will be drawn on subjects who have not donated one in a previous study. The sample for HLA and C3 may be drawn at baseline or at any subsequent visit.
- A blood sample for possible exploratory pharmacogenomic testing will be drawn at the baseline/Day 1 visit for subjects who have not had a sample collected in a previous study; however, the sample will be analyzed only if consent/assent is obtained for this optional analysis. If a blood sample is not obtained at baseline, the sample may be drawn at any time during the study following consent obtained from the subject.
- All ECGs during the study will be single assessments with the exception of baseline which will be obtained in triplicate. An ECG should be repeated for a change from baseline in QTcF &gt; 60 msec or a QTcF interval &gt; 500 msec. Prior to obtaining an ECG, subjects should rest quietly in a supine position for at least 10 minutes. ECGs should be obtained prior to any blood sampling.
- AE-QoL will be administered once at baseline and at Weeks 4, 8, 12, and every 12 weeks thereafter. The TSQM will be administered once at baseline and at Weeks 4, 8, 12, and every 12 weeks through Week 96. After Week 96, the TSQM will be administered every 24 weeks (Weeks 120, 144, 168, 192, 216, and 240). Additional questions regarding loss of time from work/school will be instituted at each visit to more fully characterize QoL. Long-term experience on study will be assessed every 24 weeks beginning at Week 96.
- Concomitant medications will be reviewed at each visit and on treatment, new concomitant medications will be reviewed to determine whether there are any contraindications or dosage adjustments required.
- The actual date and time of PK blood draw, and time of last study drug dose (relative to PK sample draw) will be captured in the CRF. Samples for PK will only be collected through the Week 48 visit.
- Site staff will review the diary instructions with the subjects at the first clinic visit. Subjects will be instructed to bring the completed diary with them to their visits. Any issues with the entered data in the diary will be discussed with the subject and corrections will be made by the subject as required.
- Sites will request a treatment allocation in the IXRS for eligible subjects at the Day 1 visit.
- At any time the diary is in a subject's possession (diary is dispensed at screening visit), they will enter HAE attacks and relevant details (screening through follow-up) and dosing information (as applicable, prior to Week 48 only) once per day. No dosing information will be collected in the diary after Week 48.
- Up to 240 weeks or until another mechanism is available to provide drug to the subject, or until the Sponsor discontinues development of the product for the prevention of angioedema attacks, whichever comes first.
L875: ## Key Inclusion Criteria
L876: - Males and females \ge12 years of age with a clinical diagnosis of type I or II HAE who have participated in a berotralstat study OR reside in countries open to recruitment of treatment-naïve subjects, and who have a clinical diagnosis of HAE type I or II, defined as:Having a C1-INH functional level below 50% and a C4 level below the LLN reference range, as assessed during the screening period OR laboratory documentation of a
L877: historical C1-INH functional level &lt;50% and a C4 level below the LLN reference range.

b. In the absence of a low C4 level, the following was acceptable to confirm the diagnosis of HAE:

- 1) SERPING-1 gene mutation known or likely to be associated with HAE type I or II assessed during the screening period; 2) a confirmed family history of C1-INH deficiency; 3) a C4 redrawn and retested during an attack with the results below the LLN reference range measured during the screening period.
L878: ## Key Exclusion Criteria
L879: - Clinically significant abnormal ECG including but not limited to a QTcF &gt;470 ms for women, a QTcF &gt;450 ms for men, a PR interval &gt;220 ms (both sexes), or ventricular and/or atrial premature contractions that are more frequent than occasional, and/or as couplets or higher in grouping.
- Known family history of sudden cardiac death.
- Calculated CLCR of ≤30 mL/min or AST or ALT value at least three-fold the ULN reference range at screening or at the last available visit prior to enrollment.
- Pregnancy or breast feeding or planned pregnancy during the study period.
- Use of any of the following prohibited medications:Angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors within 7 days of the baseline visit or planned initiation during the study (potential for exacerbation of HAE).
- Investigational drug exposure, other than berotralstat, within 30 days prior to the screening visit (or baseline if no screening visit) or initiation during the study.
- Use of a medication that is transported by P-gp and has a narrow therapeutic range within 7 days of the baseline visit or planned initiation during the study. For the purposes of this protocol, these are: aliskiren, digoxin, posaconazole, and talinolol.
- Use of a concomitant medication that is metabolized by CYP2D6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, or CYP3A4 and has a narrow therapeutic range, within 7 days of the baseline visit or planned initiation during the study, including those known to prolong the QT interval.
L880: *Reviewer comment:* The protocol initially included only subjects from prior berotralstat studies (version 6), however, was later amended (version 7) to allow enrollment of naïve patients. There was no minimum attack rate requirement for entry into the study; thus, this study population could potentially have less severe disease than the subjects in Study 302.
L881: ## Subject Removal Criteria
L882: Subjects were permanently withdrawn from the study drug if any of the following circumstances occurred:
L883: - Emergence of any laboratory abnormality or AE that in the judgment of the investigator compromised the ability of the subject to continue study-specific procedures or it was considered not to be in the subject's best interest as a result of an altered benefit-risk profile.
L884: - Recurrence of any significant treatment-emergent AST or ALT elevations or abnormalities indicative of significant hepatic toxicity.
- Subsequent determination that inclusion/exclusion criteria were not met.
- Intercurrent illness or emergence of a new illness/medical condition that, in the judgment of the investigator, affected assessment of clinical status to a significant degree.
- Subject noncompliance with study drug or the protocol.
- The subject had a QTcF &gt;500 ms (confirmed on repeat testing) or other significant QTcF changes.
- Subjects with a study drug-related grade 4 rash according to the DMID criteria.
L885: ## Study Treatments and Blinding
L886: Study treatment was not blinded. All subjects received open-label berotralstat capsules for oral administration at a dose of 110 or 150 mg QD. Subjects initially allocated to the 110 mg QD group received a daily dose as 110 mg capsules (one capsule per day). Subjects initially allocated to the 150 mg QD treatment group received a daily dose supplied as 50 mg capsules (three capsules per day) or 150 mg capsules (one capsule per day). Based on the results of Part 1 of Study 302, all subjects will be transitioned to berotralstat 150 mg QD.
L887: ## **Primary Endpoints**
L888: The primary objective of the study is to assess the long-term safety of berotralstat. The safety endpoints are as follows:
L889: - The proportion of subjects who discontinue berotralstat as a result of a TEAE.
- The proportion of subjects with treatment-emergent serious adverse events (SAEs).
- The proportion of subjects with TEAEs.
- The proportion of subjects with treatment-emergent grade 3 or 4 AEs.
- The proportion of subjects with treatment-emergent, treatment-related AE consistent with a drug rash.
- The proportion of subjects with treatment-emergent grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities.
L890: ## **Secondary and Other Endpoints**
L891: Efficacy endpoints include:
L892: - Number and rate of HAE attacks.
- Durability of response (trend in attack rate over time).
- Number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms.
- Patient-reported outcomes (HAE disease-specific AE-QoL questionnaire scores and TSQM global satisfaction scores).
- Number of attacks requiring attack medication.
- Discontinuations because of lack of efficacy (through week 48 only).
- Severity of attacks.
L893: *Reviewer comment: The results of this open label, uncontrolled study primarily serve as supportive evidence of safety. Although the study captured efficacy outcomes, we did not rely on the results to provide evidence of effectiveness for approval, and thus the efficacy results will not be covered in this review. Safety results are discussed below.*
L894: ## 8.3.7.1. Study BCX7353-204 Results
L895: This section will focus on the safety results from the 18-month interim analysis period from the date of first subject enrollment (February 27, 2018) to the data cutoff date (August 20, 2019). As mentioned above, subjects were transitioned from the 110 mg to the higher 150 mg dose following data analysis from Part 1 of Study 302. Between June 11, 2019 and August 20, 2019 (data cutoff), 22 of the 100 subjects initially allocated to the 110 mg group were transitioned to berotralstat 150 mg. However, all safety analyses were conducted based on the subject's initial treatment dose assignment. As very few TEAEs occurred in subjects after crossing over to the higher dose given the short time period between dose escalation and data cutoff, this did not have a major impact on the conduct of the safety analyses. TEAEs which occurred in subjects after the transition to the 150 mg dose are discussed under Adverse Events. beginning on 11 June 2019.
L896: ## Disposition
L897: Overall, a total of 254 subjects were screened and 227 were allocated to receive study drug—100 subjects to berotralstat 110 mg and 127 to berotralstat 150 mg (Table 49).
L898: The most common reason for screening failure was failure to meet the clinical diagnosis of HAE type I or II. Subjects were enrolled at 49 sites in 22 countries, mostly in Europe. There was a fairly high rate of early discontinuation of study drug (59 subjects [26%]). The most common reasons for discontinuation of study drug overall and prior to week 48 were perceived lack of efficacy (n=28/227 [12.3%]) and laboratory abnormalities or AEs (n=19/227 [8.4%]), respectively. Patients who stopped the study drug as a result of a perceived lack of efficacy were more likely to be in the 110 mg berotralstat group than the 150 mg berotralstat group (17 versus 11 subjects, respectively). In contrast, patients who stopped the study drug because of an AE or laboratory abnormality were more likely to be in the 150 mg berotralstat group than the 110 mg berotralstat group (13 versus 6 subjects, respectively).

L906: ## Patient Demographics
L907: Table 50 displays the demographic data of the subjects in Study 204. Similar to Study 302, there was a predominance of white, middle-aged females. Unlike Study 302, the vast majority of study sites were outside the United States. There were more adolescent subjects (n=10) than in Study 302 (n=6), providing further safety data for this subpopulation. There was a small number of elderly patients ≥65 years of age (n=5). Across the two treatment groups, there was a higher proportion of patients in the 150 mg group (56%) than the 110 mg group (44%).

L912: ## Baseline Disease Characteristics
L913: Table 51 lists the baseline characteristics of subjects in Study 204. The majority of subjects (66.5%) were enrolled directly into this study without having participated in a prior berotralstat study, and the proportion of subjects who had participated in a previous berotralstat study was higher in the 150 mg group (48%) than the 110 mg group (15%). The mean age of symptom onset and age at diagnosis is consistent with the epidemiology of the disease where symptoms commonly manifest with increasing frequency and severity around puberty and patients are diagnosed within the next few years after. A combination of abdominal and peripheral attacks being the most common locations is also consistent with the usual disease presentation. Patients in the 150mg treatment group had more laryngeal attacks within the past year and therefore this group may have had a greater disease burden at the start of the study.

L919: ## Berotralstat Exposure
L920: Table 52 shows exposure to berotralstat in Study 204. The mean total exposure to study drug was 283 days. For subjects in the berotralstat 150 mg group, the mean time exposed to study drug (307 days) was greater than that in the berotralstat 110 mg group (253 days). This discrepancy is noted by the Applicant to be due to early preferential dosing allocation to 150 mg. A substantial number (49%) of subjects in the 150 mg group received 337 to 504 days of exposure, equivalent to 1 to almost 2 years of therapy.

L924: ## Adverse Events
L925: ## Rollover Subjects
L926: A total of three subjects reported 5 TEAEs after switching from berotralstat 110 to 150 mg. However, in the tables below, these TEAEs are attributed to the 110 mg dose group because subjects were analyzed according to the initial treatment group allocation. One subject developed tendinitis, another developed a leg fracture and the third subject had three episodes of HAE attacks. The HAE attacks and the leg fracture were identified as SAEs; however, none of these five TEAEs appear related to study drug. None of the remaining 19 patients who transitioned from the 110 mg to the 150 mg dose reported TEAEs following the dose escalation. Therefore, analysis of the data by this reviewer did not reveal any difference in the safety
L927: profile of berotralstat after accounting for patients transitioned from the 110 mg to the 150 mg dose.
L928: ## Deaths
L929: No deaths were reported in the OLE Study 204.
L930: ## Nonfatal SAEs
L931: Serious nonfatal AEs that occurred during the OLE are shown in Table 53. Any SAE which occurred in 2 or more subjects is listed, as well as any single event within a SOC which was previously identified as having a potential safety signal in the pivotal study. For example, hepatobiliary disorder was still included despite only one event, due to the known GI safety profile of the drug. Most treatment-emergent SAEs were attacks of HAE (12 subjects [5.3%]), all of which required hospitalization. The 110 mg group had 10 subjects with HAE attacks with the remaining 2 in the 150 mg group, which was consistent with efficacy results favoring the 150 mg dose. GI SAEs were few—abdominal pain, elevated liver enzymes, and an abnormal liver function test (each in one subject) (Table 53). Other significant SAEs that may be relevant but occurred infrequently were two episodes of pneumonia and one of biliary colic, all in the 150 mg group. There was no clustering of SAEs in any other system organ class.

L935: ## AEs Leading to Study Drug Discontinuation
L936: TEAEs leading to premature study drug discontinuation and which occurred in 2 or more subjects or single events related to GI events or LFT labs are outlined in Table 54.
L937: Overall, 19 of 227 subjects (8.4%) discontinued study drug due to a TEAE; 6 subjects (6.0%) in the berotralstat 110 mg group and 13 subjects (10.2%) in the 150 mg group. TEAEs leading to discontinuation in at least one subject were related to GI symptoms (upper abdominal pain, vomiting) or elevated liver enzymes and were more common in the 150 mg group. All patients who discontinued study drug due to nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea were in the 150 mg group.

L942: ## Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L943: The TEAEs with a frequency of \ge 10\% in either of the berotralstat groups or overall are listed in Table 55. The safety profile was similar to that of Study 302, with most AEs in the infections and GI SOCs. Most infections were mild and transient episodes of upper respiratory tract infections and nasopharyngitis. Although diarrhea was a more common AE in the 150 mg group, abdominal pain was more common in the 110 mg group. Therefore, a clear dose-response relationship for overall GI side effects could not be established. Similar to Study 302 Part 1, high rates of GI-related AEs did not dissuade most subjects from continuing the study drug. Most of these AEs were of mild-to-moderate severity; only four subjects had a grade 3 or 4 GI TEAE. Consistent with the efficacy results from Study 302 Part 1, more HAE attacks occurred in the 110 mg versus the 150 mg dose group.

L964: The Applicant asserted that a history of prior androgen use was a risk factor for the development of elevated liver enzymes in their phase 2 and 3 studies. Although not evident in Study 302 Part 1, the relationship between ALT elevation with berotralstat treatment and history of androgen use for HAE prophylaxis is more apparent in Study 204. Elevations &gt;3× ULN only occurred in subjects who reported prior androgen therapy; however, there were insufficient data provided to determine the impact of other factors such as dose and duration of prior androgen use, time since cessation of androgen therapy, and transaminase elevation with androgen therapy to fully characterize this potential risk. Therefore, in the absence of additional data and a placebo control comparator, it is difficult to interpret this data into meaningful information that could be included in the product label to guide prescribers.
L965: Reviewer comment: Although androgen therapy is clearly associated with a higher risk of hepatocyte injury, there is no known mechanism by which prior androgen-exposed patients would be more susceptible to the hepatotoxic effects of berotralstat. A literature search was performed, and no similar relationship was found in the lanadelumab program or with other currently approved HAE therapeutic agents.
L966: ## 120-Day Safety Update
L967: The Applicant also submitted a 120-day safety update report with a data cutoff at December 1, 2019. This provided integrated safety data from all three parts of Study 302 (first patient enrolled March 14, 2018) and OL Study 204 (first patient enrolled February 27, 2018). Most common TEAEs observed in the integrated Phase 2/3 studies remained consistent with what was reported in the pivotal trial, particularly mild to moderate gastrointestinal adverse events such as abdominal pain and diarrhea. No new safety concerns were identified in laboratory safety monitoring, with the exception of a single grade 3 AE of transient leukocytosis. No TEAEs
L968: of arrhythmia or QT prolongation were reported. Overall, there is no evidence of any increasing or new toxicity with chronic use of berotralstat.
L969: ## Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L970: In Study 302, one subject in the 110 mg group developed multiple myeloma, which was deemed unlikely to be related to study treatment, and one subject in the placebo group developed a leiomyoma. In Study 204, one subject in the 150 mg group developed acute myelocytic leukemia. Although rare events such as malignancy are unlikely to appear in a clinical development program of this size, berotralstat is not anticipated to have immunomodulatory effects that increase the risk of cancer in humans. Potential tumor and cancer risks to humans based on non-clinical studies were discussed in Section 5.1.1.
L971: ## Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L972: All pregnancy tests administered during Part 1 of Study 302 were negative. Three pregnancies were reported for the OLE Study 204: Subjects [REDACTED](b) (6), and a partner pregnancy for Subject [REDACTED](b) (6). In both female subjects, study drug was stopped, and the estimated maximum drug exposure was 28 days. One female subject had an uncomplicated delivery of a healthy infant and the other had a delivery date of May 21, 2020. The male subject whose partner had become pregnant continued study drug and his partner delivered a healthy infant.
L973: ## Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L974: The safety profile in adolescent patients 12 to 17 years of age enrolled in Study 302 and OLE Study 204 was similar to that in adults. Berotralstat is not expected to impact growth. Although the consequences of long-term suppression of plasma kallikrein are not fully known, the fact that congenital deficiency of prekallikrein is largely asymptomatic is reassuring.
L975: ## Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
L976: Based on the mechanism of action, berotralstat is not expected to have any potential for drug abuse or dependence.
L977: ## 8.3.8. Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L978: ## Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarketing Experience
L979: There is no postmarketing experience with berotralstat.
L980: ## Expectations on Safety in the Postmarketing Setting
L981: Because Studies 204 and 302 excluded patients with certain levels of pre-existing LFT abnormalities, it is possible that patients with HAE with underlying hepatic dysfunction may experience more pronounced side effects not observed in clinical trials. However, the dosage
L982: recommendation for patients with moderate to severe hepatic impairment will be 110 mg (Section 6.3.3). Overall, the patient population evaluated in Study 302 as well as Study 204 are reasonably representative of the HAE patients who are expected to take berotralstat in the postmarket setting. Although the clinical trial safety database is small due to the rarity of this disease, the safety profile observed in the clinical development program may be generalized to the target population.
L983: ## 8.3.9. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L984: The safety data submitted with the NDA were sufficient to assess the safety of berotralstat in the proposed HAE population. The safety information for berotralstat is primarily derived from the pivotal, placebo-controlled phase 3 trial BCX7353-302, Part 1. Additional support for long-term safety comes from the OLE Study BCX7353-204. Study 302 included 121 adult and adolescent HAE subjects, 81 of whom received at least one dose of berotralstat and of these 40 received the proposed recommended starting dose of 150 mg once daily. A total of 227 subjects (100 in the 110 mg group and 127 in the 150 mg group) received open-label berotralstat in the OLE Study 204. No deaths occurred in the berotralstat development program. SAEs were single events, only one of which occurred with berotralstat (in Study 302) and appeared unrelated to treatment. Gastrointestinal-related adverse events and transaminase elevations emerged as the main safety signals. In Part 1 of Study 302, TEAEs such as abdominal pain, vomiting, and diarrhea occurred more frequently in a dose-dependent manner with berotralstat compared to placebo. Dyspepsia and gastroesophageal reflux events occurred with greater frequency in the 110 mg dose group and led to one treatment discontinuation. That said, GI-related TEAEs were generally mild-to-moderate severity and self-limited, and typically did not lead to study drug discontinuation. TEAEs related to transaminase elevations, primarily increased ALT, were reported with berotralstat treatment in the controlled and OLE studies. No potential Hy's law cases were identified in the program. In Study 302, only one subject had AST/ALT &gt;3\times ULN; this subject was in the 150 mg group and had ALT &gt;10\times ULN, which led to treatment discontinuation. In OLE Study 204, there was no clear dose-response with regard to AST/ALT elevations &gt;3\times ULN; however, again, one subject in the 150 mg group experienced ALT &gt;10\times ULN. Overall, though, transaminase elevations were generally transient, asymptomatic, not dose-dependent, and in some cases confounded by underlying comorbidities and prior androgen use. In general, safety data from OLE study 204 was consistent with the placebo controlled data and supports chronic berotralstat use. Although not observed in clinical trials with the 150 mg dose, berotralstat prolongs the QT interval in a dose and concentration dependent manner based on data from the thorough QT study. Therefore, based on the pharmacologic action, QT prolongation is an anticipated risk with higher than recommended doses of 150 mg once daily. Overall, the berotralstat safety profile for the treatment of a rare, serious disease such as HAE is favorable.
L985: ## 8.4. Conclusions and Recommendations
L986: The recommended regulatory action from both the clinical and statistical perspective is Approval. The berotralstat development program for HAE prophylaxis consisted of a single pivotal efficacy and safety trial, BCX7353-302, Part 1 that was adequately conducted and well-
L987: controlled. Results from this trial demonstrated substantial evidence of efficacy with both berotralstat doses compared to placebo in patients with type I or II HAE for the primary endpoint of HAE attack rate over the 24 week treatment period and pre-defined exploratory endpoint of \ge 50\% reduction in attack rate from baseline. Given the small number of adolescent subjects, efficacy in this age group is primarily based on extrapolation from efficacy data in adults because the pathophysiology of disease is similar irrespective of age. The numerically greater and more clinically significant treatment effect in the higher dose arm supports a recommended starting dose of 150 mg once daily, with dose adjustments to 110 mg once daily in patients with moderate-to-severe hepatic impairment and for patients with persistent GI-related adverse reactions to allow flexibility and individualization in dosing while maintaining similar efficacy.
L988: The safety data submitted with the NDA were sufficient to assess the safety of berotralstat in the proposed HAE population. Gastrointestinal-related adverse events and transaminase elevations emerged as the main safety signals. TEAEs such as abdominal pain, vomiting, diarrhea, dyspepsia and gastroesophageal reflux occurred more frequently, often in a dose-dependent manner, with berotralstat compared to placebo. However, GI-related TEAEs were generally mild-to-moderate severity and self-limited, and typically did not lead to study drug discontinuation. TEAEs related to transaminase elevations, primarily increased ALT, were reported with berotralstat treatment in the controlled and OLE studies. Few subjects had marked elevations triggering treatment discontinuation, and no potential Hy's law cases were identified in the program. Transaminase elevations were generally transient, asymptomatic, not dose-dependent, and in some cases confounded by underlying co-morbidities and prior androgen use. In general, safety data from OLE study 204 was consistent with the placebo controlled data and supports chronic berotralstat use. Although not observed in clinical trials, QT prolongation is an anticipated risk with higher than recommended doses based on the pharmacologic action of the drug. Efficacy and safety results across various demographic and baseline characteristic subgroups, including adolescents, were generally consistent with the overall findings. Overall, the risk/benefit profile for berotralstat in the treatment of a rare, serious disease such as HAE is favorable.
L989: ## 9. Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
L990: The Pulmonary and Allergy Drug Advisory Committee (PADAC) was not convened to discuss this NDA. Neither a PADAC meeting nor an external consultation was deemed necessary because the review did not raise any significant or unexpected safety or efficacy issues for this drug class or indication.
L991: ## 10. Pediatrics
L992: Berotralstat has been granted orphan drug designation and is exempt from the requirements of the Pediatric Research Equity Act. Adolescents were included in phase 2 and 3 trials, and therefore, the indication will be for patients with type I or II HAE who are 12 years of age and older.

L1014: ## 14. Division Director (DPACC) Comments
L1015: This NDA provides for berotralstat as a prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE in patients 12 years of age and older. Berotralstat is an NME that inhibits plasma kallikrein. The tradename is Orladeyo. HAE is a rare disease with variable attacks of swelling/edema that can potentially be life threatening. In patients with HAE, deficient or dysfunctional C1-INH leads to increased kallikrein activity and release of bradykinin which leads to the characteristic swelling of acute HAE attacks. Berotralstat decreases plasma kallikrein activity to control excess bradykinin generation in patients with HAE.
L1016: The efficacy and safety of berotralstat was evaluated in a single 24-week, randomized, double-blind, placebo controlled, clinical trial in 120 patients with HAE 12 years of age and older (Study 302). While Study 302 had several parts, the focus of the review was on Part 1, the 24-week
L1017: placebo-controlled period. Two dose levels were evaluated: 110 mg once daily and 150 mg once daily.
L1018: The primary endpoint was the rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during the 24-week treatment period. HAE attacks are an established and meaningful efficacy endpoint for HAE programs. Results from this pivotal trial showed a significant reduction in HAE attacks over the 24 week treatment period for both doses of berotralstat compared to placebo. The HAE attack rate per 28 days was 1.7, 1.3, and 2.4 for berotralstat 110 mg, berotralstat 150 mg, and placebo respectively with a rate ratio of 0.70 (95% CI 0.51, 0.95) [berotralstat 110 mg] and 0.56 (95% CI 0.41, 0.77) [berotralstat 150 mg] relative to placebo. Secondary endpoints were generally supportive of efficacy. While efficacy was demonstrated for both berotralstat doses, there was a numerically greater treatment effect with the 150 mg dose. In addition, there were numerically greater proportions of patients with 50%, 75%, and 90% reductions from baseline of investigator confirmed attack rates with the 150 mg dose compared to the 110 mg dose of berotralstat. Based upon these results, the sponsor has proposed the 150 mg dose of berotralstat. Overall, this single adequate and well-controlled trial is sufficient to provide substantial evidence of efficacy. Efficacy was demonstrated for each dose group of berotralstat and the results for the 150 mg group were statistically persuasive.
L1019: The safety data were derived primarily from the single pivotal trial, Study 302, with some supportive data from the open-label Study 204. While the size of the safety database is small, this is not unusual for a rare disease program and overall the safety database is acceptable for this indication. Review of the safety data showed that GI adverse reactions (e.g. abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea) were the most commonly reported adverse reactions and there was a dose response demonstrated. There were no deaths. SAEs were limited and didn't identify a particular safety signal. Elevated transaminases were also noted, but no cases of Hy's law were identified. QT prolongation with doses higher than 150 mg were identified in the thorough QT study. Given the nonlinear PK for berotralstat, information regarding potential for QT prolongation with higher than approved doses will be included in the product labeling.
L1020: The sponsor has proposed an indication in patients down to 12 years of age. There were only six adolescents in Study 302. Review of the efficacy and safety data for these six adolescents is consistent with the findings in adult patients. An additional 10 adolescents were evaluated in Study 204. No unique safety signals were identified. The pathophysiology of HAE is the same irrespective of age and efficacy in adolescents is expected to be similar to that in adults and may be extrapolated from the adult data.
L1021: Overall, the submitted data support a favorable benefit risk profile for berotralstat for the prevention of HAE attacks in patients 12 years of age and older. All disciplines recommend approval and I agree with approval of this application. Berotralstat provides a new oral treatment option for patients with HAE. Labeling has been agreed to by the FDA and the Applicant. The recommended dosing regimen is 150 mg once daily. A 110 mg daily dose is recommended for patients with moderate-to-severe hepatic impairment or for patients with persistent GI adverse reactions.
L1022: ## 15. Office Director (OII) Comments
L1023: I concur with the recommendation from the Division of Pulmonology, Allergy and Critical Care to approve berotralstat, an orally administered inhibitor of active plasma kallikrein, for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 12 years of age and older. This new molecular entity represents a new prophylaxis option for patients with HAE.
L1024: The efficacy of berotralstat for this rare disorder was demonstrated in a single double-blind, placebo-controlled trial. Both doses evaluated, 110 mg and 150 mg administered daily, significantly reduced investigator-confirmed HAE attack rates compared to placebo through 24 weeks of treatment. The numerically greater treatment effect supports a recommended starting dose of 150 mg, with use of 110 mg recommended for patients with moderate-to-severe hepatic impairment and for patients with persistent gastrointestinal-related adverse reactions.
L1025: Berotralstat was generally well tolerated with gastrointestinal-related adverse reactions and transaminase elevations as the main safety findings. Prolongation of the QT interval is an expected finding at doses above 150 mg based on the pharmacology of the drug. Product labeling adequately conveys the safety risks associated with use of berotralstat; a REMS will not be required.
L1026: The pathophysiology of HAE is similar in adolescents and adults. Although the number of adolescent subjects enrolled in clinical trials was small, efficacy and safety results were similar to those in adults, supporting inclusion of pediatric patients aged 12 years of age and older in the indicated population.

L1032: ## 16.2. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L1033: ## 16.2.1. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L1034: ## 16.2.1.1. Integrated Summary and Safety Evaluation
L1035: The nonclinical program of berotralstat consists of studies of pharmacology, pharmacokinetics, and toxicology. The toxicology program consists of studies of general toxicity (chronic oral toxicity studies in rats and monkeys), genetic toxicity (standard battery of testing assays), carcinogenicity (oral traditional 2-year bioassay in rats and a 26-week study in a transgenic mouse model), and reproductive and developmental toxicity (teratology studies in rats and rabbits, a fertility and early embryofetal developmental study in rats, and a peri- and post-natal development [PPND] study in rats).
L1036: ## 16.2.1.2. Pharmacology
L1037: Berotralstat decreases vascular permeability and edema in patients with hereditary angioedema (HAE) by reducing the blood level of bradykinin, a potent vasodilatory agent. Berotralstat decreases the bradykinin level by inhibiting kallikrein (inhibition constant 0.44nM), which cleaves and releases bradykinin from high-molecular-weight kininogen. The half-maximal inhibitory concentration for kallikrein activity in activated human, monkey, and rat plasma is 5.4, 5.2, and 7.2nM, respectively.
L1038: Berotralstat inhibited currents of K+, Na+, and Ca++ channels in vitro and prolonged QT intervals in monkeys and humans. It also affected duration and amplitude of action potential in vitro. The effect of berotralstat on QT has been thoroughly investigated in clinical studies. See the section on effects on the Cardiovascular System.
L1039: ## 16.2.1.3. Pharmacokinetics and Toxicokinetics
L1040: Berotralstat was rapidly absorbed after oral administration to animals. The plasma drug level generally increased in proportion to the administered dose. There were no significant difference in plasma drug level between sexes. Oral bioavailability was 33% and 45% in rats and
L1041: monkeys, respectively. The half-life was 18.5, 22.7, and 93 hours in rats, monkeys, and humans, respectively. The protein-binding capacity was high in humans, rats, and mice (98.7 to 99.8%) but low in monkeys and rabbits (74.1% to 81.9%). The tissues showing the highest maximum concentrations of radioactivity were the liver, kidney medulla, spleen, adrenal glands, kidneys, lungs, and pituitary gland.
L1042: Metabolism of berotralstat was low across species. About 61% of orally administered berotralstat was excreted unchanged in rats. Less than 10% of berotralstat was metabolized when incubated with human cryopreserved hepatocytes for 360 minutes. No unique or major human metabolites were observed.
L1043: Bioaccumulation of berotralstat occurred in rats, but not in monkeys. In rats, the berotralstat area under the curve from time zero to 24 hours (AUC_{0-24}) in week 26 was approximately three-fold that in week 1. The monkeys showed no apparent difference in AUC_{0-24} between weeks 1 and 39. For example, the respective mean AUC_{0-24} at 20 mg/kg/day in weeks 1 and 26 was 3190 and 9710 ng.h/mL in rats and 3310 and 3110 ng.h/mL in monkeys.
L1044: ## 16.2.1.4. Toxicology
L1045: A comprehensive toxicology program was conducted to evaluate the general toxicity, genetic toxicity, carcinogenicity, and reproductive and developmental toxicity of berotralstat. General toxicity studies included chronic oral toxicity studies for up to 6 and 9 months in rats and monkeys, respectively. Evaluation of the genetic and reproductive toxicity potential involved standard batteries of tests. The carcinogenicity studies comprised a standard 2-year bioassay and a study in a transgenic mouse model. A comprehensive evaluation of the effect of berotralstat on growth and development in juvenile animals has not been completed.
L1046: Evaluation of the general toxicity of berotralstat included chronic toxicity studies for up to 6 months in rats and 9 months in monkeys. These studies showed that the toxicity of berotralstat was dependent on the dose and treatment duration. The berotralstat doses used in the chronic toxicity studies were up to 20 and 80 mg/kg/day in rats and monkeys, respectively. Higher doses of berotralstat were used in the shorter-term studies (up to 75 and 100 mg/kg/day in rats and monkeys, respectively). The target organs of toxicity were the kidney, liver, intestine, and others ().

L1051: ## Mortality
L1052: Dose- and treatment duration-dependent deaths occurred in rats, but not in monkeys. Deaths in rats occurred at \ge 750 mg/kg after single doses of 150 mg/kg/day for 7 days (AUC_{0-24} 58,000 ng.h/mL), and 75 mg/kg/day for 4 weeks (AUC_{0-24} 40,500 ng.h/mL). No deaths occurred at a single dose of 450 mg/kg (AUC_{0-24} 11,900 ng.h/mL), 25 mg/kg/day for 4 weeks (AUC_{0-24} 11,200 ng.h/mL), or 20 mg/kg/day for 26 weeks (AUC_{0-24} 16,000 ng.h/mL). No deaths were observed in monkeys at doses up to 100 mg/kg/day for 28 days (AUC_{0-24} 16,000 ng.h/mL).
L1053: ## Studies in Rats
L1054: The repeat-dose studies in Wistar Han rats involved oral (gavage) administration of berotralstat for 4, 13, and 26 weeks. The berotralstat dose was up to 75, 20, and 20 mg/kg/day in the 4-, 13-, and 26-week studies, respectively. The results identified the following as the target organs of berotralstat toxicity: liver, kidney, and intestine.
L1055: In a 4-week study (#806-131), rats (10 and 5/sex/dose in the main study and recovery period, respectively) were dosed with 0 (water), 5, 10, 25, and 75 mg/kg/day once daily for 28 days. The recovery period was 2 weeks. Additional rats (three and nine of each sex in the vehicle and treatment groups, respectively) were included in the toxicokinetics (TK) analysis. The mean AUC_{0-24, \text{day } 28, \text{male/female}} was 1310, 3830, 20,200, and 130,000 ng.h/mL in the low-dose (LD), mid-low-dose, mid-high-dose, and high-dose (HD) groups, respectively. The HD rats showed audible breathing and piloerection, decreased bodyweight (9% to 16%), clinical chemistry changes (decreased mean corpuscular volume (MCV) and mean corpuscular hemoglobin (MCH) and an increased number of neutrophils, aspartate aminotransferase [AST] level, and alanine aminotransferase [ALT] level), and increased liver weight. The microscopic changes included vacuolation/foamy macrophages in multiple organs (the adrenal glands [cortical], brain [choroid plexus], liver, lung, lymph nodes [mesenteric], ovaries, pituitary glands, salivary glands, and intestine); tubular regeneration in the kidney; and minimal degeneration/necrosis and hypertrophy of the bile ducts. Most of the microscopic changes were confined to the HD group, but liver and bile duct changes were observed in all of the groups. The findings in the LD groups were of low incidence and minimal severity.
L1056: In a 13-week study (#806-144), rats (15 and 5/sex/dose in the main study and recovery period, respectively) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 2.5, 7.5, and 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 26 weeks. The recovery period was 6 weeks. Additional rats (12/sex/dose) were included in the TK analysis. The respective mean AUC_{0-24, \text{day } 90} in the LD, MD, and HD groups was 318, 1890, and 6330 ng.h/mL in males; and 476, 3480, and 16,000 ng.h/mL in females. The MD and HD groups showed minimal to mild bile duct hypertrophy in males and females (incidence 10 MD males, 14 HD males, 15 MD females, and 15 HD females). The HD group showed bile duct hyperplasia (6 females only) and vacuolated macrophages (13 and 15 males and females, respectively). The no observable adverse effects level [NOAEL] of the study was 2.5 mg/kg/day, corresponding to a mean AUC_{0-24} of 397 ng.h/mL.
L1057: In a 26-week study (#8001291), rats (15 and 5/sex/dose in the main study and recovery section, respectively) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 1.0, 2.5, 7.5, and 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for
L1058: 26 weeks. Additional rats (nine/sex/dose) were included in the TK analysis. The recovery period was 13 weeks in duration. The mean AUC_{0-24, \text{day } 182, \text{male/female}} was 56.9, 297, 2030, and 9710 ng.h/mL in the LD, mid-low-dose, mid-high-dose, and HD groups, respectively. Microscopic changes were observed in the hepatobiliary system, lymph nodes (mesenteric), spleen, intestine (jejunum and duodenum), and nasal cavity in the \ge 7.5 mg/kg/day groups. Most of the changes were vacuolation in the mucosa and foamy histiocytes. The bile duct showed minimal-to-moderate hyperplasia. Changes in the nasal cavity were believed to be related to gastric reflux and irritation. The NOAEL of the study was 2.5 mg/kg/day, corresponding to a mean AUC_{0-24} of 297 ng.h/mL.
L1059: ## Studies in Monkeys
L1060: Studies completed in nonhuman primates involved oral (gavage) administration of berotralstat for 4, 13, and 39 weeks in cynomolgus monkeys. In the 4-week study (Study 806-132), monkeys (five and two/sex/dose in the control, MD, and HD groups in the main study and recovery period, respectively; and three/sex in the MD main study) were dosed with 0, 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 28 days. The mean AUC_{0-24, \text{day } 28, \text{male/female}} was 1350, 5320, and 146,000 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. The HD group showed emesis and watery feces; changes in electrocardiogram parameters; increases in lipase (&gt;7-fold), AST (~3-fold), and ALT (6- to 10-fold) levels, increases in the organ weight of the kidney and liver; and microscopic changes. Microscopic changes were observed in the kidney (basophilic tubules and tubular degeneration/regeneration), liver (diffused fatty changes and increased cytoplasmic collagen and hepatocellular hypertrophy), intestine (histiocytic infiltration), and spleen (follicular lymphoid hyperplasia). No significant findings were observed in the recovery animals after the 3-week recovery period.
L1061: In a 13-week study (Study 806-145), monkeys (four and two/sex/dose in main study and recovery sections, respectively) were dosed with 0, 2.5, 7.5, and 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 91 days. The recovery period was 6 weeks. The mean AUC_{0-24, \text{day } 91, \text{male/female}} was 278, 930, and 3350 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. No treatment-related findings were observed in any berotralstat treatment groups. The NOAEL was 20 mg/kg/day.
L1062: In a 39-week study (Study 8001292), monkeys (four and two/sex/dose in the main study and recovery section, respectively) were dosed with 0, 2.5, 7.5, and 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 273 days. The recovery period was 13 weeks. The mean AUC_{0-24, \text{day } 273, \text{male/female}} was 240, 559, and 2880 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. No treatment-related findings were observed in any of the berotralstat treatment groups. The NOAEL was 20 mg/kg/day.
L1063: In another 39-week study (#806-162), monkeys (6, 4, 4, and 6/sex in the C, LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 30, 55, and 80 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 273 days. Additional monkeys were included for a 13-week interim analysis (three/sex/dose) and recovery (two/sex/dose in the control and HD groups only). The mean AUC_{0-24, \text{day } 273, \text{male/female}} was 3950, 7780, and 13,500 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. The MD and HD groups showed decreases (up to 38%) in fibrinogen concentration, increases in AST and ALT levels (up to 4- and 8.7-fold, respectively) and a decrease in albumin
L1064: level (16%). The histological findings in the MD and HD groups were as follows (Table 60): degeneration (kidney, tubular), hyperplasia (kidney, bone marrow, and spleen), hypertrophy (liver, hepatocellular), vacuolation (adrenal, cortical), and foamy macrophages (lung, lymph node, and intestine). The foamy macrophages were a result of phospholipidosis. The LD group showed foamy macrophages in the intestine and lymph nodes. The kidney showed the most severe tissue damage (tubular degeneration and hyperplasia), but the intestine was the most sensitive organ. The findings were most pronounced in the main study and were of lesser magnitude in the interim and recovery sections. The results indicate that the effects of berotralstat in monkeys were partially reversible. This study did not establish a NOAEL.

L1069: ## Targets of Berotralstat Toxicity
L1070: ## Kidney
L1071: The kidneys are target organs of berotralstat toxicity in both rats and monkeys. Dose-dependent increases in the incidence of tubular degeneration and regeneration were observed at 75 mg/kg/day berotralstat in the 4-week rat study and at \ge 55 mg/kg/day berotralstat in the 39-week study in monkeys. The finding was absent at 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat in the 26-week study in rats and at 30 mg/kg/day berotralstat in the 39-week study in monkeys.
L1072: ## Phospholipidosis
L1073: Phospholipidosis was a consistent finding in rats and monkeys (Section 5.1). Phospholipidosis, however, was not observed in humans in clinical trials (refer to the end-of-phase 2 internal meeting held on October 25, 2017).
L1074: ## Cardiovascular System
L1075: Berotralstat caused dose- and plasma-concentration-dependent increases in the QT interval in monkeys and humans. See Section 5.1 and the interdisciplinary review team review completed by Dr. Girish Bende et al. on April 15, 2020 (DARRTS ID 4592746).
L1076: ## Safety Margins
L1077: Animal toxicity studies generally provided safety margins of greater than one for relevant findings in general toxicity studies on a plasma drug AUC basis (Table 61). Some safety margins were smaller than 1 (i.e., 0.1 to 0.8), but these were generally irrelevant, being attributable to phospholipidosis, which was absent in humans. The safety margins for reproductive and developmental toxicity and carcinogenicity are discussed in the labeling review.

L1084: ## Genetic Toxicity and Carcinogenicity
L1085: See Section 5.1.
L1086: ## Reproductive Toxicity
L1087: A standard battery of tests for reproductive and developmental toxicity of berotralstat did not reveal any significant treatment effect (Table 62). The battery included a fertility and early embryonic developmental study in rats, embryofetal development (EFD) studies in rats and rabbits (one each), and a PPND toxicity study in rats. None of the studies showed findings of significant concern. Berotralstat was nonteratogenic under the test conditions and did not affect fertility or PPND in rats.
L1088: Table 62. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity Studies of Berotralstat
L1089: Category	Species	Treatment	n/Dose	Berotralstat (mg/kg/day)	Study #
Fertility	Rat	Prematinga to GD 7	25	0, 10, 25, 45	806-156
Rat	6 to 17	21-23	0, 10, 25, 75	9000644
EFD	Rabbit	7 to 19	20-21	0, 20, 50, 100	9000815

L1092: b Dosing ended on GD 6 in females and the pups were culled to 4 and 1 on postnatal days 4 and 28, respectively.
L1093: Abbreviations: EFD, embryofetal development; GD, gestation day; LAD, lactation day; PPND, peri- and post-natal development
L1094: In a fertility study (Study #806-156), male and female Sprague-Dawley rats (25/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 10, 25, and 45 mg/kg/day berotralstat. The treatment started 4 weeks prior to mating and continued through and after mating for a total of 9 weeks. The treatment in females started 2 weeks prior to mating and continued until gestational day
L1095: (GD) 7. Males were evaluated for fertility and sperm quality (day 63). Females were evaluated for fertility and uterine contents on GD 13. The HD group showed increases in the incidence of audible breathing in both sexes (incidence three and six male and female rats, respectively). The HD males showed a statistically significant decrease in bodyweight at the end of treatment (5%, p&lt;0.05) and a decrease in food consumption (up to 14%, p&lt;0.01). The mean plasma drug level was 835 and 795 ng/mL in males and females at the last day of treatment (on treatment day 63 and GD 7, respectively). No treatment-related effects were observed in any of the fertility parameters in either sex and in the sperm analysis in males. Berotralstat did not affect fertility in male or female rats under the test conditions.
L1096: In an EFD study in rats (Study #9000644), pregnant dams (21 to 23/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 10, 25, and 75 mg/kg/day berotralstat during the organogenesis period (GD 6 to 17). They were euthanized on GD 21 for examination of uterine contents. Four (of twenty-three) HD dams showed audible breathing. The HD group showed a statistically significant decrease in mean bodyweight gain (34%, p&lt;0.01). No treatment-related effects were observed in any of the fetal structural parameters evaluated. The mean AUC on GD 17 was 3510, 12,400, and 24,600 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. Berotralstat was nonteratogenic in Sprague-Dawley rats under the test conditions.
L1097: In an EFD study (Study #9000815), pregnant New Zealand white rabbit does were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 20, 50, and 100 mg/kg/day berotralstat during the organogenesis period (GD 7 to 19). They were euthanized on GD 29 for the examination of uterine contents. The mean plasma AUC on day 19 was 880, 2350, and 4410 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. The HD group showed statistically significant decreases in bodyweight gain and food consumption (100% and 32%, respectively; p&lt;0.05). The HD group had abortions and early terminations (two rabbits each). These findings were not considered of concern because they were associated with significant maternal toxicity (i.e., significant decreases in bodyweight). No treatment-related effects were observed in any other fetal development parameters. Berotralstat can cross the placenta; the drug was present in fetal blood at up to 11% of the concentration in maternal blood. Berotralstat was nonteratogenic in New Zealand white rabbits under the test conditions.
L1098: In a PPND study in rats (Study #806-157), dams (F_0) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 10, 25, and 45 mg/kg/day berotralstat during pregnancy and lactation (i.e., from GD 6 to lactation day [LAD] 20). The respective mean plasma berotralstat concentration in the LD, MD, and HD dams was 46.2, 183.3, and 312.8 on GD 6 and 63.8, 360.3, and 699 ng/mL on LAD 14. Dams gave births to and nurtured pups (F_1) naturally. The berotralstat concentration in pup plasma on LAD 14 was &lt;5% of the maternal maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}). Pups grew and matured naturally and were assessed for growth and behavioral development parameters (e.g., motor activity, learning, and memory) while growing. The F_1 mated at the age of 10 weeks and pregnant females were euthanized on GD 13 for the examination of uterine contents. Both dams and pups (F_0 and F_1, respectively) in the HD group showed lower bodyweight gain during the lactation period (up to 7.8%, p&lt;0.01). The dams also showed a statistically significant decrease in food consumption (up to 10%). Therefore, berotralstat did not affect PPND parameters in rats under the test conditions. The NOAEL was 25 mg/kg/day.
L1099: ## Juvenile Animal Study
L1100: No comprehensive toxicity study in juvenile animals has been completed to evaluate the effect of berotralstat on growth and development. The Applicant states that they evaluated the effect of berotralstat on growth and development in a PPND study in rats (#806-157). This review disagrees with the Applicant based on the limitation of the design of the PPND study. Specifically, pups received limited exposure through milk during the lactation period and no drug exposure postweaning. During the lactation period, the plasma berotralstat level in pups was less than 5% that in maternal blood. Such limited exposure to berotralstat cannot fully evaluate its effect on the growth and development of rat pups.
L1101: The lack of a juvenile animal study does not preclude approval for the current indication. The Applicant stated that the animals used in their toxicity program were comparable to adolescent humans. Specifically, dosing in the 26-week study in rats was initiated at 10 weeks of age. The 39-week toxicity studies in monkeys commenced dosing at a median age of 2 years 10 months to 3 years 3 months. The human ages equivalent to those of the rats and monkeys are approximately 14 and 12 years, respectively. The Applicant should consider a well-designed study to fully evaluate the effect of berotralstat on growth and development in juvenile animals if they intend to develop berotralstat for younger pediatric patients in the future.
L1102: ## Drug Product Impurities
L1103: There are no safety concerns regarding impurities in the drug product. Six impurities were identified and discussed in the new drug application—[REDACTED] (b) (4). The specifications of these impurities were \le [REDACTED] \mug/day (b) (4).
L1104: These specifications are considered adequate based on the discussion below.
L1105: Safety evaluation of the impurities included structural activity analysis for genetic toxicity and evaluation of impurity levels in the general toxicity studies. Bacterial gene reverse mutation assays (Ames test) were performed for impurities with structural alerts. For impurities with no structural alerts, group features present in the parent compound, and specifications of &gt; [REDACTED] %, safety evaluations were conducted by comparing their levels to those in 13-week repeat-dose toxicity studies.
L1106: Bacterial gene reverse mutation (Ames) assays were performed on [REDACTED] (b) (4). Only [REDACTED] (b) (4) tested positive (in only the TA98 test strain). The level of [REDACTED] (b) (4) will be kept below the lower limit of quantitation of [REDACTED] ppm. The total daily intake of [REDACTED] (b) (4) in patients receiving 150 mg/day berotralstat will be below the safety concern threshold of [REDACTED] \mug (Source: electronic Common technical Document 3.2.S.3.2, subsection 2.3.4, Table 73, p. 154). [REDACTED] (b) (4) considered qualified based on their lack of mutagenicity and the specification (not more than [REDACTED] %).
L1107: Additional safety evaluations were conducted for the following three impurities: [REDACTED] (b) (4) whose specifications were greater than [REDACTED] %. Table 63 lists the daily intake of these impurities and the corresponding safety margins. The Applicant argued that
L1108: these impurities are qualified because they were present at similar levels in the 13-week toxicity studies in rats and monkeys (Studies 806-144 and 806-145, respectively). Both of these studies revealed no significant toxicity at the highest dose of 20 mg/kg/day. The monkey study provided a safety margin of at least 2 based on exposure calculated as mg/m2.

L1117: The nonclinical review team consulted the chemistry review team regarding the structural alerts of the impurities and the Applicant's safety assessment (via email dated May 8, 2020). Dr. Lawrence Perez found no structural alerts of potential genotoxicity for any of the three impurities (source, email on May 8, 2020). Further, these compounds have groups present in berotralstat and are considered nonmutagenic per the International Council for Harmonisation-M7 guidance. Because these compounds were present at the proposed levels in repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and monkeys, these impurities are considered qualified.
L1118: ## Conclusion
L1119: The Applicant has conducted adequate nonclinical characterization and safety evaluation of berotralstat. Findings in the general toxicity studies of berotralstat in animals have been adequately monitored and addressed in clinical trials. Genetic toxicity, carcinogenicity, and reproductive and developmental toxicity studies identified no safety signal of concern. The reviewer recommends approval of the drug from the nonclinical perspective.
L1120: ## 16.3. OCP Appendices (Technical Documents Supporting OCP Recommendations)
L1121: ## 16.3.1. Population Pharmacokinetics Analysis
L1122: ## 16.3.1.1. Review Summary
L1123: In general, the Applicant's population pharmacokinetics (PK) analysis is considered acceptable to support the claims (Table 64). The Applicant's analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant discordance identified.
L1124: **Table 64. Specific Comments on the Applicant's Final Population PK Model**
L1125: Utility of the Final Model	Reviewer's Comments
Support labeling statements about intrinsic factors	
"Body weight, age, gender, and race did not have a clinically meaningful influence on the systemic exposure of berotralstat."
"Based on the population pharmacokinetic analyses that included elderly patients (\ge 65 to 74 years, N=25), age does not have a clinically meaningful impact on the systemic exposure of berotralstat."
The statement is acceptable. Covariate analysis by the Applicant demonstrates no evident difference based on age, gender, and ethnicity. The higher exposure in lighter patients is not expected to cause meaningful QT prolongation, so no dose adjustment is recommended.

Derive exposure metrics in pediatric population for the labeling	
"Based on population pharmacokinetic analyses that included pediatric patients 12 to <18 years of age, exposure at steady state following oral administration of berotralstat 150 mg once daily was approximately 20% higher (b) (4) compared to adults."
The Applicant's final model is acceptable for generating exposure metrics in pediatric patients aged 12 to 18 years (Table 68).

L1128: ## Introduction
L1129: The primary objectives of the Applicant's analysis were to:
L1130: - Characterize the structural PK model and quantify the population variability in the PK parameters of berotralstat.
- Evaluate the effects of intrinsic factors on berotralstat exposure.
L1131: ## Model Development
L1132: ## Data

The reasons for exclusion of PK samples were predose observation, missing date/time, PK without dosing, and outliers. The final Nonlinear Mixed-Effects Modeling data file for analysis contained 10,437 PK observations from 771 subjects. Table 66 provides summary statistics of the baseline demographic covariates in the analysis dataset.

L1144: ## Base Model
L1145: The base model for berotralstat was a linear three-compartment model with first-order absorption, an absorption lag time, and linear elimination (Figure 10). An effect of dose on relative bioavailability was included in the base model to address the nonlinear PK of berotralstat. Interindividual variability was modelled assuming a log-normal distribution for patient-level random effects. Residual variability was tested as additive, proportional, or both on the dependent variable. The stochastic approximation/expectation maximization (SAEM) method was used for parameter estimation, and the relative standard error (RSE) was used for hypothesis testing, with a forward-addition RSE of 0.510 (p=0.05) and a backward-elimination RSE of 0.388 (p=0.005). The SAEM was used because it can handle complex models with many parameters.
L1146: Figure 10. Diagram of the Base Model
L1147: Source: Revised from Figure 9 of the Applicant's Population PK report (BCX7353-PPK-01-A2 Addendum).
L1148: ## Covariate Analysis
L1149: Clinical judgment, physiologic relevance, and mechanistic plausibility were used to determine which covariates should be tested with the various PK parameters. Additionally, the collinearity of covariates was assessed to ensure that no collinear covariates were added to the model. The effects of the covariate parameters body weight, age, gender, and ethnicity on apparent oral clearance (CL/F) and apparent central volume (V_c/F); bilirubin on CL/F; and food effect on the absorption rate (K_a) were tested in the covariate analysis. Continuous covariates were
L1150: evaluated using a power function and categorical covariates were parameterized as a fractional change.
L1151: ## Final Model
L1152: The final model (Model 258) was a three-compartment model with first-order absorption with an absorption lag time and linear elimination. The effect of between-subject variability on clearance, volume of distribution, K_a, and absorption lag time was evaluated. A between-study random effect on clearance was included. Bodyweight was the only covariate for clearance and volume. An effect of dose on relative bioavailability was included to address the nonlinear PK of berotralstat (higher dose predicting higher bioavailability). Residual variability was modeled by a combined additive and proportional component.
L1153: The parameter estimates for the final model are listed in Table 67, and the goodness-of-fit plots in Figure 11. Selected visual predictive check plots for the final model are shown in Figure 12. Figure 13 shows that inclusion of bodyweight in the final model accounted for the trend between the random effects of berotralstat PK parameters and bodyweight in the base model.

L1169: ## Simulations
L1170: Simulations based on the final model were conducted by the Applicant to predict the PK profiles and parameters of the following populations at a dose of 150 mg once daily, using the
L1171: (b) (4)-capsule formulation:
L1172: - Adolescents (12 to 18 years of age)
- Underweight adults (40 to 60 kg)
- Low-weight adults (60 to 80 kg)
- Normal-weight adults (80 to 100 kg)
L1173: - Overweight adults (100 to 120 kg)
L1174: For adult subjects, the mean and variance for weight were derived from the data available in the current dataset. Simulated weight values were generated on the log-transformed values for weight normal distribution.
L1175: For the adolescent population, bodyweight and the variance thereof were derived from the Weight-for-Age Tables of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention and log-transformed. Simulated weight values for adolescents were obtained from a normal distribution.
L1176: There were 1000 simulated subjects in each population generated. The predicted median PK profiles of the specified weight groups after reaching steady state following berotralstat 150 mg daily dosing are shown in Figure 14. Steady-state AUC0-24 and Cmax values are listed in Table 68.
